href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Telephone_numbers_in_Niger" title="Telephone numbers in Niger">Calling code</a></th><td code</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/ISO_3166-2:NE" title="ISO 3166-2:NE">NE</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Country_code_top-level_domain" title="Country code top-level domain">Internet TLD</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/.ne" title=".ne">.ne</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Niger</b> or <b>the Niger</b><sup id="cite_ref-nameISO_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nameISO-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-nameUNGEGN_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nameUNGEGN-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/iː/: &#39;ee&#39; in &#39;fleece&#39;">iː</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="/ʒ/: &#39;s&#39; in &#39;pleasure&#39;">ʒ</span><span title="/ɛər/: &#39;are&#39; in &#39;bare&#39;">ɛər</span></span>,<span class="wrap"> </span><span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/aɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;tide&#39;">aɪ</span><span title="/dʒ/: &#39;j&#39; in &#39;jam&#39;">dʒ</span><span title="/ər/: &#39;er&#39; in &#39;letter&#39;">ər</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling">nee-<span style="font-size:90%">ZHAIR</span>, <span style="font-size:90%">NY</span>-jər</i></a>,<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> <small>French:&#160;</small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="fr-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/French" title="Help:IPA/French">[niʒɛʁ]</a></span>),<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> officially the <b>Republic of the Niger</b><sup id="cite_ref-nameISO_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nameISO-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-nameUNGEGN_14-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nameUNGEGN-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> (<a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a>: <i lang="fr">République du Niger</i>, <a href="/wiki/Hausa_language" title="Hausa language">Hausa</a>: <i lang="ha">Jamhuriyar Nijar</i>), is a <a href="/wiki/Landlocked_country" title="Landlocked country">landlocked country</a> in <a href="/wiki/West_Africa" title="West Africa">West Africa</a>. It is a <a href="/wiki/Unitary_state" title="Unitary state">unitary state</a> <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Niger#Political_geography" title="Geography of Niger">bordered</a> by <a href="/wiki/Libya" title="Libya">Libya</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Libya%E2%80%93Niger_border" title="Libya–Niger border">northeast</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chad" title="Chad">Chad</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Chad%E2%80%93Niger_border" title="Chad–Niger border">east</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nigeria" title="Nigeria">Nigeria</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Niger%E2%80%93Nigeria_border" title="Niger–Nigeria border">south</a>, <a href="/wiki/Benin" title="Benin">Benin</a> and <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso" title="Burkina Faso">Burkina Faso</a> to the southwest, <a href="/wiki/Mali" title="Mali">Mali</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Mali%E2%80%93Niger_border" title="Mali–Niger border">west</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Algeria" title="Algeria">Algeria</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Algeria%E2%80%93Niger_border" title="Algeria–Niger border">northwest</a>. It covers a land area of almost 1,270,000&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (490,000&#160;sq&#160;mi), making it the second-largest landlocked country in <a href="/wiki/West_Africa" title="West Africa">West Africa</a>, after <a href="/wiki/Chad" title="Chad">Chad</a>. Over 80% of its land area lies in the <a href="/wiki/Sahara" title="Sahara">Sahara</a>. Its predominantly <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_Niger" title="Islam in Niger">Muslim</a> population of about 25 million<sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_2022_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> live mostly in clusters in the further south and west of the country. The capital <a href="/wiki/Niamey" title="Niamey">Niamey</a> is located in Niger's southwest corner.
</p><p>It is listed in the <a href="/wiki/Least_developed_countries" title="Least developed countries">least developed countries</a> group. Some non-desert portions of the country underwent periodic drought and <a href="/wiki/Desertification" title="Desertification">desertification</a>. The economy is concentrated around <a href="/wiki/Subsistence_agriculture" title="Subsistence agriculture">subsistence agriculture</a>, with some export agriculture in the more fertile south, and export of raw materials, including <a href="/wiki/Uranium_ore" title="Uranium ore">uranium ore</a>. It faces challenges to development due to its landlocked position, desert terrain, higher <a href="/wiki/Fertility_rate" class="mw-redirect" title="Fertility rate">fertility rates</a> without <a href="/wiki/Birth_control" title="Birth control">birth control</a> and resulting <a href="/wiki/Population_growth" title="Population growth">population growth</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-EconPop_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EconPop-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The society reflects a diversity drawn from the independent histories of some ethnic groups and regions and their period living in a single state. Historically, Niger has been on the fringes of some states. Since independence, Nigeriens have lived under 5 constitutions and 3 periods of <a href="/wiki/Military_dictatorship" title="Military dictatorship">military rule</a>. After the military coup in 2010, Niger became a <a href="/wiki/Democracy" title="Democracy">democratic</a>, multi-party state. A <a href="/wiki/Majority" title="Majority">majority</a> of the population lives in rural areas.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Etymology">Etymology</span></h2>
<p>The name comes from the <a href="/wiki/Niger_River" title="Niger River">Niger River</a> which flows through the west of the country; a theory about the origin of the river's name is that it comes from the <a href="/wiki/Tuareg_languages" title="Tuareg languages">Tuareg</a> <i>n'eghirren</i> meaning 'flowing water'.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The pronunciation is the French one of <span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/iː/: &#39;ee&#39; in &#39;fleece&#39;">iː</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="/ʒ/: &#39;s&#39; in &#39;pleasure&#39;">ʒ</span><span title="/ɛər/: &#39;are&#39; in &#39;bare&#39;">ɛər</span></span>/</a></span></span>, while in Anglophone media <span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/aɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;tide&#39;">aɪ</span><span title="/dʒ/: &#39;j&#39; in &#39;jam&#39;">dʒ</span><span title="/ər/: &#39;er&#39; in &#39;letter&#39;">ər</span></span>/</a></span></span> is also used.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Niger" title="History of Niger">History of Niger</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Prehistory">Prehistory</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:1997_278-10_Sahara_glyph.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/1997_278-10_Sahara_glyph.jpg/220px-1997_278-10_Sahara_glyph.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="138" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/1997_278-10_Sahara_glyph.jpg/330px-1997_278-10_Sahara_glyph.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/1997_278-10_Sahara_glyph.jpg/440px-1997_278-10_Sahara_glyph.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1680" data-file-height="1050" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:1997_278-10_Sahara_glyph.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Rock engraving showing herds of giraffe, ibex, and other animals in the southern Sahara near Tiguidit, Niger</div></div></div>
<p>Stone tools, some dating as far back as 280,000 BC, have been found in <a href="/wiki/Adrar_Bous" title="Adrar Bous">Adrar Bous</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bilma" title="Bilma">Bilma</a> and <a href="/wiki/Djado" title="Djado">Djado</a> in the northern <a href="/wiki/Agadez_Region" title="Agadez Region">Agadez Region</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Some of these finds have been linked with the <a href="/wiki/Aterian" title="Aterian">Aterian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mousterian" title="Mousterian">Mousterian</a> tool cultures of the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Paleolithic" title="Middle Paleolithic">Middle Paleolithic</a> period, which flourished in northern Africa circa 90,000 BC–20,000 BC.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> It is thought that these humans lived a hunter-gatherer lifestyle.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> In prehistoric times the climate of the <a href="/wiki/Sahara" title="Sahara">Sahara</a> was wetter and more fertile, a phenomenon archaeologists refer to as the '<a href="/wiki/Green_Sahara" class="mw-redirect" title="Green Sahara">Green Sahara</a>' which provided "favourable" conditions for hunting and later agriculture and livestock herding.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Neolithic" title="Neolithic">Neolithic</a> era began circa 10,000 BC; this period saw a number of changes, such as the introduction of <a href="/wiki/Pottery" title="Pottery">pottery</a> (as evidenced at Tagalagal, Temet and Tin Ouffadene), the spread of cattle husbandry, and the burying of the dead in stone <a href="/wiki/Tumuli" class="mw-redirect" title="Tumuli">tumuli</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> As the climate changed in the period 4000–2800 BC the Sahara gradually began <a href="/wiki/Desertification" title="Desertification">drying out</a>, forcing a change in settlement patterns to the south and east.<sup id="cite_ref-larousse-history_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-larousse-history-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Agriculture spread, including the planting of <a href="/wiki/Millet" title="Millet">millet</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sorghum" title="Sorghum">sorghum</a>, and pottery production.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Iron and copper items appear in this era, with finds including those at <a href="/wiki/Azawagh" title="Azawagh">Azawagh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Takedda" title="Takedda">Takedda</a>, <a href="/wiki/Marendet" title="Marendet">Marendet</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Termit_Massif" title="Termit Massif">Termit Massif</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Kiffian_culture" title="Kiffian culture">Kiffian</a> (circa 8000–6000 BC) and later <a href="/wiki/Tenerian_culture" title="Tenerian culture">Tenerian</a> (circa 5000–2500 BC) cultures, centred on <a href="/wiki/Adrar_Bous" title="Adrar Bous">Adrar Bous</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gobero" title="Gobero">Gobero</a> where skeletons have been uncovered, flourished during this period.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Clark_&amp;_DGG_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Clark_&amp;_DGG-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-scidaily_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-scidaily-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-nytimes_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nytimes-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Societies continued to grow with regional differentiation in agricultural and funerary practices. A culture of this period is the <a href="/wiki/Bura_culture" title="Bura culture">Bura culture</a> (circa 200–1300 AD) named for the <a href="/wiki/Bura_archaeological_site" title="Bura archaeological site">Bura archaeological site</a>. where a burial replete with iron and ceramic statuettes were discovered.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The Neolithic era saw the flourishing of Saharan rock art, including in the <a href="/wiki/A%C3%AFr_Mountains" title="Aïr Mountains">Aïr Mountains</a>, Termit Massif, Djado Plateau, Iwelene, Arakao, Tamakon, Tzerzait, <a href="/wiki/Iferouane" title="Iferouane">Iferouane</a>, Mammanet and <a href="/wiki/Dabous_Giraffes" title="Dabous Giraffes">Dabous</a>; the art spans the period from 10,000BC to 100AD and depicts a range of subjects, from the varied fauna of the landscape to depictions of spear-carrying figures dubbed 'Libyan warriors'.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Shillington_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Shillington-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Empires_and_kingdoms_in_pre-colonial_Niger">Empires and kingdoms in pre-colonial Niger</span></h3>
<p>By at least the 5th century BC the territory of what later is Niger had become an area of trans-Saharan trade. Led by <a href="/wiki/Tuareg" class="mw-redirect" title="Tuareg">Tuareg</a> tribes from the north, camels were used as a means of transportation through what is later a desert.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Masonen_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Masonen-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> This mobility which would continue in waves for centuries was accompanied with further migration to the south and intermixing between sub-Saharan African and North African populations, and the spread of <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> It was aided by the Arab invasion of North Africa in the 7th century, which resulted in population movements to the south.<sup id="cite_ref-larousse-history_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-larousse-history-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Empires and kingdoms existed in the Sahel during this era. The following adopts a roughly chronological account of some empires.
</p>
<h4><span id="Mali_Empire_.281200s.E2.80.931400s.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Mali_Empire_(1200s–1400s)">Mali Empire (1200s–1400s)</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Mali_Empire" title="Mali Empire">Mali Empire</a></div>
<p>The Mali Empire was a <a href="/wiki/Mandinka_people" title="Mandinka people">Mandinka</a> empire founded by <a href="/wiki/Sundiata_Keita" title="Sundiata Keita">Sundiata Keita</a> (r. 1230–1255) in circa 1230 and existed up to 1600. As detailed in the <i><a href="/wiki/Epic_of_Sundiata" title="Epic of Sundiata">Epic of Sundiata</a></i>, Mali emerged as a breakaway region of the <a href="/wiki/Sosso_Empire" title="Sosso Empire">Sosso Empire</a> which itself had split from the earlier <a href="/wiki/Ghana_Empire" title="Ghana Empire">Ghana Empire</a>. Thereafter Mali defeated the Sosso at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Kirina" title="Battle of Kirina">Battle of Kirina</a> in 1235 and then Ghana in 1240.<sup id="cite_ref-oxfordre1_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oxfordre1-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> From its heartland around the later Guinea-Mali border region, the empire expanded under successive kings and came to dominate the Trans-Saharan trade routes, reaching its greatest extent during the rule of <a href="/wiki/Mansa_Musa" title="Mansa Musa">Mansa Musa</a> (r. 1312–1337).<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> At this point parts of what are now Niger's <a href="/wiki/Tillab%C3%A9ri_Region" title="Tillabéri Region">Tillabéri Region</a> fell under Malian rule.<sup id="cite_ref-oxfordre1_42-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oxfordre1-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> A Muslim, Mansa Musa performed the <i><a href="/wiki/Hajj" title="Hajj">hajj</a></i> in 1324–25 and encouraged the spread of <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a> in the empire, and it "appears that most ordinary citizens continued to maintain their traditional animist beliefs instead of or alongside the new religion".<sup id="cite_ref-oxfordre1_42-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oxfordre1-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ancient1_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ancient1-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> The empire began "declining" in the 15th century due to a combination of internecine strife over the royal succession, weak kings, the shift of European trade routes to the coast, and rebellions in the empire's periphery by <a href="/wiki/Mossi_people" title="Mossi people">Mossi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Wolof_people" title="Wolof people">Wolof</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tuareg" class="mw-redirect" title="Tuareg">Tuareg</a> and <a href="/wiki/Songhai_people" title="Songhai people">Songhai</a> peoples.<sup id="cite_ref-ancient1_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ancient1-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> A rump Mali kingdom continued to exist until 1600s.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Songhai_Empire_.281000s.E2.80.931591.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Songhai_Empire_(1000s–1591)">Songhai Empire (1000s–1591)</span></h4>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:SONGHAI_empire_map.PNG" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/SONGHAI_empire_map.PNG/220px-SONGHAI_empire_map.PNG" decoding="async" width="220" height="138" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/SONGHAI_empire_map.PNG/330px-SONGHAI_empire_map.PNG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/SONGHAI_empire_map.PNG/440px-SONGHAI_empire_map.PNG 2x" data-file-width="1580" data-file-height="988" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:SONGHAI_empire_map.PNG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Map of the Songhai Empire, overlaid over modern boundaries</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Songhai_Empire" title="Songhai Empire">Songhai Empire</a></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Songhai_Empire" title="Songhai Empire">Songhai Empire</a> was named for its main ethnic group, the <a href="/wiki/Songhai_people" title="Songhai people">Songhai or Sonrai</a>, and was centred on the bend of the <a href="/wiki/Niger_River" title="Niger River">Niger River</a> in <a href="/wiki/Mali" title="Mali">Mali</a>. Songhai began settling this region from the 7th to 9th centuries;<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> by the 11th century <a href="/wiki/Gao" title="Gao">Gao</a> (capital of the former <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Gao" class="mw-redirect" title="Kingdom of Gao">Kingdom of Gao</a>) had become the empire's capital.<sup id="cite_ref-boubou-hama_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-boubou-hama-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-countrystudy_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-countrystudy-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> From 1000 to 1325, the Songhai Empire managed to maintain peace with the Mali Empire, its neighbour to the west. In 1325 Songhai was conquered by Mali until regaining its independence in 1375.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Under king <a href="/wiki/Sonni_Ali" title="Sonni Ali">Sonni Ali</a> (r. 1464–1492) Songhai adopted an expansionist policy which reached its apogee during the reign of <a href="/wiki/Askia_Mohammad_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Askia Mohammad I">Askia Mohammad I</a> (r. 1493–1528); at this point the empire had expanded from its Niger-bend heartland, including to the east where most of later western Niger fell under its rule, including <a href="/wiki/Agadez" title="Agadez">Agadez</a> which was conquered in 1496.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The empire was unable to withstand repeated attacks from the <a href="/wiki/Saadi_dynasty" class="mw-redirect" title="Saadi dynasty">Saadi dynasty</a> of <a href="/wiki/Morocco" title="Morocco">Morocco</a> and was decisively defeated at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tondibi" title="Battle of Tondibi">Battle of Tondibi</a> in 1591; it then collapsed into a number of smaller kingdoms.<sup id="cite_ref-countrystudy_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-countrystudy-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Sultanate_of_A.C3.AFr_.281400s.E2.80.931906.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Sultanate_of_Aïr_(1400s–1906)">Sultanate of Aïr (1400s–1906)</span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:1997_277-9A_Agadez_mosque_cropped.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/1997_277-9A_Agadez_mosque_cropped.jpg/220px-1997_277-9A_Agadez_mosque_cropped.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="148" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/1997_277-9A_Agadez_mosque_cropped.jpg/330px-1997_277-9A_Agadez_mosque_cropped.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/1997_277-9A_Agadez_mosque_cropped.jpg/440px-1997_277-9A_Agadez_mosque_cropped.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1072" data-file-height="723" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:1997_277-9A_Agadez_mosque_cropped.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Agadez_Mosque" title="Agadez Mosque">The Grand Mosque of Agadez</a></div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Sultanate_of_A%C3%AFr" class="mw-redirect" title="Sultanate of Aïr">Sultanate of Aïr</a></div>
<p>In c. 1449 in the north of what is now Niger, the <a href="/wiki/Sultanate_of_A%C3%AFr" class="mw-redirect" title="Sultanate of Aïr">Sultanate of Aïr</a> was founded by Sultan Ilisawan, based in <a href="/wiki/Agadez" title="Agadez">Agadez</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Formerly a trading post inhabited by a mixture of Hausa and Tuaregs, it grew as a strategic position on the Trans-Saharan trade routes. In 1515 Aïr was conquered by Songhai, remaining a part of that empire until its collapse in 1591.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The following centuries, it "seems that the sultanate entered a decline" marked by internecine wars and clan conflicts.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> When Europeans began exploring the region in the 19th century most of Agadez lay in ruins and was taken over by the French (<i>see below</i>).<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Kanem.E2.80.93Bornu_Empire_.28700s.E2.80.931700s.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Kanem–Bornu_Empire_(700s–1700s)">Kanem–Bornu Empire (700s–1700s)</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Kanem%E2%80%93Bornu_Empire" title="Kanem–Bornu Empire">Kanem–Bornu Empire</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sultanate_of_Damagaram" title="Sultanate of Damagaram">Sultanate of Damagaram</a></div>
<p>To the east, the <a href="/wiki/Kanem%E2%80%93Bornu_Empire" title="Kanem–Bornu Empire">Kanem–Bornu Empire</a> dominated the region around <a href="/wiki/Lake_Chad" title="Lake Chad">Lake Chad</a> for a period.<sup id="cite_ref-countrystudy_46-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-countrystudy-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> It was founded by the <a href="/wiki/Zaghawa_people" title="Zaghawa people">Zaghawa</a> around the 8th century and based in <a href="/wiki/Njimi" title="Njimi">Njimi</a>, north-east of the lake. The kingdom gradually expanded, including during the rule of the <a href="/wiki/Sayfawa_dynasty" title="Sayfawa dynasty">Sayfawa dynasty</a> which began in c. 1075 under <i>Mai</i> (king) <a href="/wiki/Hummay" title="Hummay">Hummay</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ancient2_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ancient2-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> The kingdom reached its greatest extent in the 1200s, partly due to the effort of <i>Mai</i> <a href="/wiki/Dunama_Dibbalemi" class="mw-redirect" title="Dunama Dibbalemi">Dunama Dibbalemi</a> (r. 1210–1259), and grew "richer" from its control of some Trans-Saharan trade routes; most of eastern and south-eastern Niger, including <a href="/wiki/Bilma" title="Bilma">Bilma</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kaouar" title="Kaouar">Kaouar</a>, was under Kanem's control in this period.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Islam had been introduced to the kingdom by Arab traders from the 11th century, gaining more converts over the following centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-ancient2_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ancient2-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Attacks by the <a href="/wiki/Bulala" class="mw-redirect" title="Bulala">Bulala</a> people in the 14th century forced Kanem to shift westwards of Lake Chad where it became known as the Bornu Empire ruled from its capital <a href="/wiki/Ngazargamu" title="Ngazargamu">Ngazargamu</a> on what is later the <a href="/wiki/Niger-Nigeria_border" class="mw-redirect" title="Niger-Nigeria border">Niger-Nigeria border</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ancient2_49-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ancient2-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Bornu "prospered" during the rule of <i>Mai</i> <a href="/wiki/Idris_Alooma" title="Idris Alooma">Idris Alooma</a> (r. circa 1575–1610) and re-conquered most of the "traditional lands" of Kanem, hence the designation 'Kanem–Bornu' for the empire. By the 17th century and into the 18th the Bornu kingdom had entered a "period of decline", shrinking back to its Lake Chad heartland.<sup id="cite_ref-countrystudy_46-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-countrystudy-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ancient2_49-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ancient2-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Circa 1730–40 a group of <a href="/wiki/Kanuri_people" title="Kanuri people">Kanuri</a> settlers led by Mallam Yunus left Kanem and founded the <a href="/wiki/Sultanate_of_Damagaram" title="Sultanate of Damagaram">Sultanate of Damagaram</a>, centred on the town of <a href="/wiki/Zinder" title="Zinder">Zinder</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The sultanate remained nominally subject to the <a href="/wiki/Borno_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Borno Empire">Borno Empire</a> until the reign of Sultan Tanimoune Dan Souleymane in the 19th century, who declared independence and initiated a phase of expansion.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The sultanate managed to resist the advance of the Sokoto Caliphate (<i>see below</i>), and was later captured by the French in 1899.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="The_Hausa_states_and_other_smaller_kingdoms_.281400s.E2.80.931800s.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Hausa_states_and_other_smaller_kingdoms_(1400s–1800s)">The Hausa states and other smaller kingdoms (1400s–1800s)</span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Photo1906_Zinder_overview.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Photo1906_Zinder_overview.jpg/220px-Photo1906_Zinder_overview.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="143" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Photo1906_Zinder_overview.jpg/330px-Photo1906_Zinder_overview.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Photo1906_Zinder_overview.jpg/440px-Photo1906_Zinder_overview.jpg 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="333" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Photo1906_Zinder_overview.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Overlooking the town of <a href="/wiki/Zinder" title="Zinder">Zinder</a> and the Sultan's Palace from the French fort (1906). The arrival of the French spelled an end for precolonial states like the <a href="/wiki/Sultanate_of_Damagaram" title="Sultanate of Damagaram">Sultanate of Damagaram</a> which carried on only as ceremonial "chiefs" appointed by the colonial government.</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Hausa_Kingdoms" title="Hausa Kingdoms">Hausa Kingdoms</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dosso_Kingdom" title="Dosso Kingdom">Dosso Kingdom</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Dendi_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Dendi Kingdom">Dendi Kingdom</a></div>
<p>Between the Niger River and Lake Chad lay <a href="/wiki/Hausa_Kingdoms" title="Hausa Kingdoms">Hausa Kingdoms</a>, encompassing the cultural-linguistic area known as <a href="/wiki/Hausaland" class="mw-redirect" title="Hausaland">Hausaland</a> which straddles what later is the <a href="/wiki/Niger-Nigeria_border" class="mw-redirect" title="Niger-Nigeria border">Niger-Nigeria border</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ancient3_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ancient3-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> The Hausa are thought to be a mixture of autochthonous peoples and migrant peoples from the north and/or east, emerging as a distinct people sometime in the 900s–1400s when the kingdoms were founded.<sup id="cite_ref-ancient3_54-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ancient3-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> They gradually adopted Islam from the 14th century, and sometimes this existed alongside other religions, developing into syncretic forms; some Hausa groups such as the Azna resisted Islam altogether (the area of <a href="/wiki/Dogondoutchi" title="Dogondoutchi">Dogondoutchi</a> remains an animist stronghold).<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-countrystudy_46-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-countrystudy-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> The Hausa kingdoms were not a compact entity but several federations of kingdoms more or less independent of one other. Their organisation was hierarchical and somewhat democratic: the Hausa kings were elected by the notables of the country and could be removed by them.<sup id="cite_ref-boubou-hama_45-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-boubou-hama-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> The Hausa Kingdoms began as seven states founded, according to the <a href="/wiki/Bayajidda" title="Bayajidda">Bayajidda</a> legend, by the six sons of Bawo.<sup id="cite_ref-ancient3_54-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ancient3-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-countrystudy_46-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-countrystudy-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Bawo was the only son of the Hausa queen <a href="/wiki/Daurama" title="Daurama">Daurama</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bayajidda" title="Bayajidda">Bayajidda</a> or (<a href="/wiki/Abu_Yazid" title="Abu Yazid">Abu Yazid</a> according to certain historians) who came from <a href="/wiki/Baghdad" title="Baghdad">Baghdad</a>. The seven original Hausa states (also referred to as the 'Hausa bakwai') were: <a href="/wiki/Daura_Emirate" title="Daura Emirate">Daura</a> (state of queen <a href="/wiki/Daurama" title="Daurama">Daurama</a>), <a href="/wiki/Sultanate_of_Kano" title="Sultanate of Kano">Kano</a>, <a href="/wiki/Rano" title="Rano">Rano</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zazzau" title="Zazzau">Zaria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gobir" title="Gobir">Gobir</a>, <a href="/wiki/Katsina" title="Katsina">Katsina</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hadejia" title="Hadejia">Biram</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-boubou-hama_45-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-boubou-hama-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> An extension of the legend states that Bawo had a further seven sons with a concubine, who went on to the found the so-called 'Banza (<i>illegitimate</i>) Bakwai': <a href="/wiki/Zamfara" class="mw-redirect" title="Zamfara">Zamfara</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kebbi_Emirate" title="Kebbi Emirate">Kebbi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nupe_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Nupe Kingdom">Nupe</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gwari" class="mw-redirect" title="Gwari">Gwari</a>, <a href="/wiki/Yauri_Emirate" title="Yauri Emirate">Yauri</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ilorin_Emirate" title="Ilorin Emirate">Ilorin</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kwararafa" title="Kwararafa">Kwararafa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_55-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> A smaller state not fitting into this scheme was <a href="/wiki/Konni_(Hausa_state)" title="Konni (Hausa state)">Konni</a>, centred on <a href="/wiki/Birni-N%27Konni" title="Birni-N&#39;Konni">Birni-N'Konni</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Fulani" class="mw-redirect" title="Fulani">Fulani</a> (also called Peul, Fulbe etc.), a pastoral people found throughout the Sahel, began migrating to Hausaland during the 1200s–1500s.<sup id="cite_ref-countrystudy_46-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-countrystudy-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ancient3_54-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ancient3-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> During the later 18th century some Fulani were unhappy with the syncretic form of Islam practised there; exploiting also the populace's disdain with corruption amongst the Hausa elite, the Fulani scholar <a href="/wiki/Usman_Dan_Fodio" class="mw-redirect" title="Usman Dan Fodio">Usman Dan Fodio</a> (from Gobir) declared a <a href="/wiki/Fulani_jihad" class="mw-redirect" title="Fulani jihad">jihad</a> in 1804.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-fisher1975_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fisher1975-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> After conquering most of Hausaland (and not the Bornu Kingdom which remained independent) he proclaimed the <a href="/wiki/Sokoto_Caliphate" title="Sokoto Caliphate">Sokoto Caliphate</a> in 1809.<sup id="cite_ref-ancient3_54-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ancient3-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Some of the Hausa states survived by fleeing south, such as the Katsina who moved to <a href="/wiki/Maradi,_Niger" title="Maradi, Niger">Maradi</a> in the south of what later is Niger.<sup id="cite_ref-countrystudy_46-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-countrystudy-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Some of these surviving states harassed the Caliphate and a period of wars and skirmishes commenced, with some states (such as Katsina and Gobir) maintaining independence whereas elsewhere newer ones were formed (such as the <a href="/wiki/Sultanate_of_Tessaoua" class="mw-redirect" title="Sultanate of Tessaoua">Sultanate of Tessaoua</a>). The Caliphate managed to survive until, "fatally weakened" by the invasions of Chad-based warlord <a href="/wiki/Rabih_az-Zubayr" title="Rabih az-Zubayr">Rabih az-Zubayr</a>, it finally fell to the British in 1903, with its lands later being partitioned between Britain and France.<sup id="cite_ref-Falola-2_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Falola-2-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Other smaller kingdoms of the period include the <a href="/wiki/Dosso_Kingdom" title="Dosso Kingdom">Dosso Kingdom</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Zarma_people" title="Zarma people">Zarma</a> polity founded in 1750 which resisted the rule of Hausa and Sokoto states.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Colonial_.281900.E2.80.9358.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Colonial_(1900–58)">Colonial (1900–58)</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Senegambia_and_Niger" title="Senegambia and Niger">Senegambia and Niger</a>, <a href="/wiki/Upper_Senegal_and_Niger" title="Upper Senegal and Niger">Upper Senegal and Niger</a>, <a href="/wiki/French_West_Africa" title="French West Africa">French West Africa</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Colony_of_Niger" title="Colony of Niger">Colony of Niger</a></div>
<p>In the 19th century some European explorers travelled in the area of what later is Niger, such as <a href="/wiki/Mungo_Park_(explorer)" title="Mungo Park (explorer)">Mungo Park</a> (in 1805–06), the <a href="/wiki/Walter_Oudney" title="Walter Oudney">Oudney</a>-<a href="/wiki/Dixon_Denham" title="Dixon Denham">Denham</a>-<a href="/wiki/Hugh_Clapperton" title="Hugh Clapperton">Clapperton</a> expedition (1822–25), <a href="/wiki/Heinrich_Barth" title="Heinrich Barth">Heinrich Barth</a> (1850–55; with <a href="/wiki/James_Richardson_(explorer)" title="James Richardson (explorer)">James Richardson</a> and <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Overweg" title="Adolf Overweg">Adolf Overweg</a>), <a href="/wiki/Friedrich_Gerhard_Rohlfs" title="Friedrich Gerhard Rohlfs">Friedrich Gerhard Rohlfs</a> (1865–67), <a href="/wiki/Gustav_Nachtigal" title="Gustav Nachtigal">Gustav Nachtigal</a> (1869–74) and <a href="/wiki/Parfait-Louis_Monteil" title="Parfait-Louis Monteil">Parfait-Louis Monteil</a> (1890–92).<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Some European countries already possessed littoral colonies in Africa, and in the latter half of the century they began to turn their eyes towards the interior of the continent. This process, known as the '<a href="/wiki/Scramble_for_Africa" title="Scramble for Africa">Scramble for Africa</a>', culminated in the <a href="/wiki/Berlin_Conference" title="Berlin Conference">1885 Berlin conference</a> in which the colonial powers outlined the division of Africa into spheres of influence. As a result of this, <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a> gained control of the upper valley of the <a href="/wiki/Niger_River" title="Niger River">Niger River</a> (roughly equivalent to the areas of what later is <a href="/wiki/Mali" title="Mali">Mali</a> and Niger).<sup id="cite_ref-IBS093_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IBS093-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> France then set about making a reality of their rule on the ground. In 1897 the French officer <a href="/wiki/Marius_Gabriel_Cazemajou" title="Marius Gabriel Cazemajou">Marius Gabriel Cazemajou</a> was sent to Niger; he reached the <a href="/wiki/Sultanate_of_Damagaram" title="Sultanate of Damagaram">Sultanate of Damagaram</a> in 1898 and stayed in <a href="/wiki/Zinder" title="Zinder">Zinder</a> at the court of Sultan Amadou Kouran Daga—he was later killed as Daga feared he would ally with the Chad-based warlord Rabih az-Zubayr.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In 1899–1900 France coordinated three expeditions—the <a href="/wiki/%C3%89mile_Gentil" title="Émile Gentil">Gentil Mission</a> from <a href="/wiki/French_Congo" title="French Congo">French Congo</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Am%C3%A9d%C3%A9e-Fran%C3%A7ois_Lamy" title="Amédée-François Lamy">Foureau-Lamy Mission</a> from <a href="/wiki/Algeria" title="Algeria">Algeria</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Voulet%E2%80%93Chanoine_Mission" title="Voulet–Chanoine Mission">Voulet–Chanoine Mission</a> from <a href="/wiki/Timbuktu" title="Timbuktu">Timbuktu</a>—with the aim of linking France's African possessions.<sup id="cite_ref-IBS093_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IBS093-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> The three eventually met at <a href="/wiki/Kouss%C3%A9ri" title="Kousséri">Kousséri</a> (in the further north of <a href="/wiki/Cameroon" title="Cameroon">Cameroon</a>) and defeated Rabih az-Zubayr's forces at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Kouss%C3%A9ri" title="Battle of Kousséri">Battle of Kousséri</a>. The Voulet-Chanoine Mission was "marred by atrocities", and "became notorious" for pillaging, looting, raping and killing local civilians on its passage throughout southern Niger.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> On 8 May 1899, in retaliation for the resistance of queen <a href="/wiki/Sarraounia" title="Sarraounia">Sarraounia</a>, captain Voulet and his men murdered all the inhabitants of the village of <a href="/wiki/Birni-N%27Konni" title="Birni-N&#39;Konni">Birni-N'Konni</a> in what is regarded as "one of the worst massacres in French colonial history".<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The "brutal" methods of Voulet and Chanoine caused a "scandal" and Paris was forced to intervene; when Lieutenant-Colonel <a href="/wiki/Jean-Fran%C3%A7ois_Klobb" title="Jean-François Klobb">Jean-François Klobb</a> caught up with the mission near <a href="/wiki/Tessaoua" title="Tessaoua">Tessaoua</a> to relieve them of command he was killed. Lt. <a href="/wiki/Paul_Joalland" title="Paul Joalland">Paul Joalland</a>, Klobb's former officer, and Lt. <a href="/wiki/Octave_Meynier" title="Octave Meynier">Octave Meynier</a> eventually took over the mission following a mutiny in which Voulet and Chanoine were killed.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Military Territory of Niger was subsequently created within the <a href="/wiki/Upper_Senegal_and_Niger" title="Upper Senegal and Niger">Upper Senegal and Niger</a> colony (what later is Burkina Faso, Mali and Niger) in December 1904 with its capital at <a href="/wiki/Niamey" title="Niamey">Niamey</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Niger-Nigeria_border" class="mw-redirect" title="Niger-Nigeria border">border</a> with Britain's colony of Nigeria to the south was finalised in 1910, a rough delimitation having already been agreed by the two powers via treaties during the period 1898–1906.<sup id="cite_ref-IBS093_58-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IBS093-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> The capital of the territory was moved to Zinder in 1912 when the Niger Military Territory was split off from Upper Senegal and Niger, before being moved back to Niamey in 1922 when Niger became a fully-fledged colony within <a href="/wiki/French_West_Africa" title="French West Africa">French West Africa</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The borders of Niger were drawn up in stages and had been fixed at their later position by the 1930s. Territorial adjustments took place in this period: the areas west of the Niger river were <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso%E2%80%93Niger_border" title="Burkina Faso–Niger border">attached</a> to Niger in 1926–27, and during the dissolution of Upper Volta (modern Burkina Faso) in 1932–47 most of the east of that territory was added to Niger;<sup id="cite_ref-IBS146_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IBS146-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> and in the east the <a href="/wiki/Tibesti_Mountains" title="Tibesti Mountains">Tibesti Mountains</a> were <a href="/wiki/Chad-Niger_border" class="mw-redirect" title="Chad-Niger border">transferred</a> to Chad in 1931.<sup id="cite_ref-IBS73_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-IBS73-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The French generally adopted a form of indirect rule, allowing existing native structures to continue to exist within the colonial framework of governance providing that they acknowledged French supremacy.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The Zarma of the Dosso Kingdom in particular proved amenable to French rule, using them as allies against the encroachments of Hausa and other nearby states; over time the Zarma thus became one of the "more educated and westernised" groups in Niger.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Perceived threats to French rule, such as the Kobkitanda rebellion in <a href="/wiki/Dosso_Region" title="Dosso Region">Dosso Region</a> (1905–06), led by the blind cleric Alfa Saibou, and the Karma revolt in the Niger valley (December 1905–March 1906) led by Oumarou Karma were suppressed with force, as were the latter <a href="/wiki/Hamallayya" title="Hamallayya">Hamallayya</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hauka" title="Hauka">Hauka</a> religious movements.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> While "largely successful" in subduing the "sedentary" populations of the south, the French faced "considerably more difficulty" with the Tuareg in the north (centered on the Sultanate of Aïr in Agadez), and France was unable to occupy Agadez until 1906.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Tuareg resistance continued, culminating in the <a href="/wiki/Kaocen_revolt" title="Kaocen revolt">Kaocen revolt</a> of 1916–17, led by <a href="/wiki/Ag_Mohammed_Wau_Teguidda_Kaocen" title="Ag Mohammed Wau Teguidda Kaocen">Ag Mohammed Wau Teguidda Kaocen</a>, with backing from the <a href="/wiki/Senussi" class="mw-redirect" title="Senussi">Senussi</a> in <a href="/wiki/Fezzan" title="Fezzan">Fezzan</a>; the revolt was violently suppressed and Kaocen fled to Fezzan where he was later killed.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> A puppet sultan was set up by the French and the "decline and marginalisation" of the north of the colony continued, exacerbated by a series of droughts.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> While it remained "something of a backwater", some limited economic development took place in Niger during the colonial years, such as the introduction of <a href="/wiki/Hausa_groundnut" class="mw-redirect" title="Hausa groundnut">groundnut</a> cultivation.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Measures to improve food security following a series of devastating famines in 1913, 1920 and 1931 were introduced.<sup id="cite_ref-Bradt_22-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bradt-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the <a href="/wiki/Second_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Second World War">Second World War</a>, during which time mainland France was occupied by <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Nazi Germany</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle" title="Charles de Gaulle">Charles de Gaulle</a> issued the Brazzaville Declaration, declaring that the French colonial empire would be replaced post-war with a less centralised <a href="/wiki/French_Union" title="French Union">French Union</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> The French Union which lasted from 1946 to 1958 conferred a limited form of French citizenship on the inhabitants of the colonies, with some decentralisation of power and limited participation in political life for local advisory assemblies. It was during this period that the <a href="/wiki/Nigerien_Progressive_Party_%E2%80%93_African_Democratic_Rally" title="Nigerien Progressive Party – African Democratic Rally">Nigerien Progressive Party</a> (<i>Parti Progressiste Nigérien</i>, or PPN, originally a branch of the African Democratic Rally, or <i><a href="/wiki/Rassemblement_D%C3%A9mocratique_Africain" title="Rassemblement Démocratique Africain">Rassemblement Démocratique Africain</a></i> – RDA) was formed under the leadership of former teacher <a href="/wiki/Hamani_Diori" title="Hamani Diori">Hamani Diori</a>, and the left-wing <a href="/wiki/Mouvement_Socialiste_Africain-Sawaba" class="mw-redirect" title="Mouvement Socialiste Africain-Sawaba">Mouvement Socialiste Africain-Sawaba</a> (MSA) led by <a href="/wiki/Djibo_Bakary" title="Djibo Bakary">Djibo Bakary</a>. Following the Overseas Reform Act (<i>Loi Cadre</i>) of 23 July 1956 and the establishment of the <a href="/wiki/Fifth_French_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Fifth French Republic">Fifth French Republic</a> on 4 December 1958, Niger became an autonomous state within the <a href="/wiki/French_Community" title="French Community">French Community</a>. On 18 December 1958, an autonomous Republic of Niger was officially created under the leadership of Hamani Diori. MSA was banned in 1959 for its perceived excessive anti-French stance.<sup id="cite_ref-djibo03_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-djibo03-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> On 11 July 1960, Niger decided to leave the French Community and acquired full independence at midnight, local time, on 3 August 1960;<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Diori thus became the first <a href="/wiki/List_of_Presidents_of_Niger" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Presidents of Niger">president</a> of the country.
</p>
<h3><span id="Post-colonial_.281960.E2.80.93.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Post-colonial_(1960–)">Post-colonial (1960–)</span></h3>
<h4><span id="Diori_years_.281960.E2.80.9374.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Diori_years_(1960–74)">Diori years (1960–74)</span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:202px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_B_145_Bild-F028554-0009,_Niger,_Staatsbesuch_Bundespr%C3%A4sident_L%C3%BCbke.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Bundesarchiv_B_145_Bild-F028554-0009%2C_Niger%2C_Staatsbesuch_Bundespr%C3%A4sident_L%C3%BCbke.jpg/200px-Bundesarchiv_B_145_Bild-F028554-0009%2C_Niger%2C_Staatsbesuch_Bundespr%C3%A4sident_L%C3%BCbke.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="144" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Bundesarchiv_B_145_Bild-F028554-0009%2C_Niger%2C_Staatsbesuch_Bundespr%C3%A4sident_L%C3%BCbke.jpg/300px-Bundesarchiv_B_145_Bild-F028554-0009%2C_Niger%2C_Staatsbesuch_Bundespr%C3%A4sident_L%C3%BCbke.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Bundesarchiv_B_145_Bild-F028554-0009%2C_Niger%2C_Staatsbesuch_Bundespr%C3%A4sident_L%C3%BCbke.jpg/400px-Bundesarchiv_B_145_Bild-F028554-0009%2C_Niger%2C_Staatsbesuch_Bundespr%C3%A4sident_L%C3%BCbke.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="576" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Bundesarchiv_B_145_Bild-F028554-0009,_Niger,_Staatsbesuch_Bundespr%C3%A4sident_L%C3%BCbke.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>President <a href="/wiki/Hamani_Diori" title="Hamani Diori">Hamani Diori</a> and visiting <a href="/wiki/German_President" class="mw-redirect" title="German President">German President</a> <a href="/wiki/Heinrich_L%C3%BCbke" title="Heinrich Lübke">Heinrich Lübke</a> greet crowds on a state visit to Niamey, 1969. Diori's single party rule was characterised by "good" relations with the West and a preoccupation with foreign affairs.</div></div></div>
<p>For its first 14 years as an independent state Niger was run by a single-party civilian regime under the presidency of Hamani Diori.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> The 1960s saw an expansion of the education system and some limited economic development and industrialisation.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Links with France remained, with Diori allowing the development of French-led <a href="/wiki/Uranium" title="Uranium">uranium</a> mining in <a href="/wiki/Arlit" title="Arlit">Arlit</a> and supporting France in the <a href="/wiki/Algerian_War" title="Algerian War">Algerian War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Relations with other African states were mostly "positive", with the exception of <a href="/wiki/Dahomey" title="Dahomey">Dahomey</a> (Benin), owing to a <a href="/wiki/Benin-Niger_border" class="mw-redirect" title="Benin-Niger border">border</a> dispute. Niger remained a one-party state throughout this period, with Diori surviving a planned coup in 1963 and an assassination attempt in 1965; most of this activity was masterminded by Djibo Bakary's MSA-Sawaba group which had launched an abortive rebellion in 1964.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> In the 1970s, a combination of economic difficulties, <a href="/wiki/Drought" title="Drought">droughts</a> and accusations of rampant corruption and mismanagement of food supplies resulted in a <a href="/wiki/1974_Nigerien_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="1974 Nigerien coup d&#39;état">coup d'état</a> that overthrew the Diori regime.
</p>
<h4><span id="First_military_regime_.281974.E2.80.931991.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="First_military_regime_(1974–1991)">First military regime (1974–1991)</span></h4>
<p>The coup had been masterminded by Col. <a href="/wiki/Seyni_Kountch%C3%A9" title="Seyni Kountché">Seyni Kountché</a> and a military group under the name of the <i>Conseil Militaire Supreme</i>, with Kountché going on to rule the country until his death in 1987.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The first action of the military government was to address the food crisis.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Whilst political prisoners of the Diori regime were released after the coup, political and individual freedoms "in general deteriorated" during this period. There were attempted coups (in 1975, 1976 and 1984) which were thwarted, their instigators being punished.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Kountché sought to create a 'development society', funded mostly by the uranium mines in <a href="/wiki/Agadez_Region" title="Agadez Region">Agadez Region</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Parastatal" class="mw-redirect" title="Parastatal">Parastatal</a> companies were created, infrastructure (building and new roads, schools, health centres) constructed, and there was corruption in government agencies, which Kountché did not hesitate to punish.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> In the 1980s Kountché began cautiously loosening the grip of the military, with some relaxation of state censorship and attempts made to 'civilianise' the regime.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The economic boom ended following the collapse in uranium prices, and <a href="/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund" title="International Monetary Fund">IMF</a>-led austerity and privatisation measures provoked opposition by some Nigeriens.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In 1985 a Tuareg revolt in <a href="/wiki/Tchintabaraden" title="Tchintabaraden">Tchintabaraden</a> was suppressed.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Kountché died in November 1987 from a brain tumour, and was succeeded by his chief of staff, Col. <a href="/wiki/Ali_Saibou" title="Ali Saibou">Ali Saibou</a> who was confirmed as Chief of the Supreme Military Council four days later.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Saibou curtailed the most repressive aspects of the Kountché era (such as the secret police and media censorship), and set about introducing a process of political reform under the overall direction of a single party (the <i>Mouvement National pour la Société du Développement</i>, or MNSD).<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> A Second Republic was declared and a new constitution was drawn up, which was adopted following a <a href="/wiki/1989_Nigerien_constitutional_referendum" title="1989 Nigerien constitutional referendum">referendum</a> in 1989.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> General Saibou became the first president of the Second Republic after winning the <a href="/wiki/1989_Nigerien_general_election" title="1989 Nigerien general election">presidential election</a> on 10 December 1989.<sup id="cite_ref-auto1_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto1-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>President Saibou's efforts to control political reforms failed in the face of trade union and student demands to institute a <a href="/wiki/Multi-party_democracy" class="mw-redirect" title="Multi-party democracy">multi-party democratic system</a>. On 9 February 1990, a violently repressed student march in Niamey led to the death of three students, which led to increased national and international pressure for further democratic reform.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The Saibou regime acquiesced to these demands by the end of 1990.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Meanwhile, trouble re-emerged in Agadez Region when a group of armed Tuaregs attacked the town of Tchintabaraden (seen by some as the start of the first <a href="/wiki/Tuareg_rebellion_(1990%E2%80%931995)" title="Tuareg rebellion (1990–1995)">Tuareg Rebellion</a>), prompting a military crackdown which led to deaths (the precise numbers are disputed, with estimates ranging from 70 to up to 1,000).<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:152px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ali_Saibou_cropped.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Ali_Saibou_cropped.jpg/150px-Ali_Saibou_cropped.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="225" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Ali_Saibou_cropped.jpg/225px-Ali_Saibou_cropped.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Ali_Saibou_cropped.jpg/300px-Ali_Saibou_cropped.jpg 2x" data-file-width="585" data-file-height="878" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ali_Saibou_cropped.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Ali Saibou, President 1987–93, helped oversee the transition from military to civilian rule</div></div></div>
<h4><span id="National_Conference_and_Third_Republic_.281991.E2.80.931996.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="National_Conference_and_Third_Republic_(1991–1996)">National Conference and Third Republic (1991–1996)</span></h4>
<p>The National Sovereign Conference of 1991 brought about multi-party democracy. From 29 July to 3 November, a national conference gathered together all elements of society to make recommendations for the future direction of the country. The conference was presided over by Prof. <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Salifou" title="André Salifou">André Salifou</a> and developed a plan for a <a href="/wiki/Transitional_government" class="mw-redirect" title="Transitional government">transitional government</a>; this was then installed in November 1991 to manage the affairs of state until the institutions of the Third Republic were put into place in April 1993. After the National Sovereign Conference, the transitional government drafted a constitution that eliminated the previous single-party system of the 1989 Constitution and guaranteed more freedoms. The new constitution was adopted by a <a href="/wiki/1992_Nigerien_constitutional_referendum" title="1992 Nigerien constitutional referendum">referendum</a> on 26 December 1992.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> Following this, presidential <a href="/wiki/1993_Nigerien_parliamentary_election" title="1993 Nigerien parliamentary election">elections</a> were held and <a href="/wiki/Mahamane_Ousmane" title="Mahamane Ousmane">Mahamane Ousmane</a> became the first president of the Third Republic on 27 March 1993.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-auto1_69-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto1-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> Ousmane's presidency saw four government changes and legislative <a href="/wiki/1995_Nigerien_parliamentary_election" title="1995 Nigerien parliamentary election">elections</a> in 1995, and an economic slump.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The violence in Agadez Region continued during this period, prompting the Nigerien government to sign a truce with Tuareg rebels in 1992 which was ineffective owing to internal dissension within the Tuareg ranks.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Another rebellion, led by dissatisfied <a href="/wiki/Toubou" class="mw-redirect" title="Toubou">Toubou</a> peoples claiming that, like the Tuareg, the Nigerien government had neglected their region, broke out in the east of the country.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In April 1995 a peace deal with a Tuareg rebel group was signed, with the government agreeing to absorb some former rebels into the military and, with French assistance, help others return to a productive civilian life.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Second_military_regime_and_third_military_regime_.281996.E2.80.931999.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_military_regime_and_third_military_regime_(1996–1999)">Second military regime and third military regime (1996–1999)</span></h4>
<p>The governmental paralysis prompted the military to intervene; on 27 January 1996, Col. <a href="/wiki/Ibrahim_Bar%C3%A9_Ma%C3%AFnassara" title="Ibrahim Baré Maïnassara">Ibrahim Baré Maïnassara</a> led a <a href="/wiki/1996_Nigerien_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="1996 Nigerien coup d&#39;état">coup</a> that deposed President Ousmane and ended the Third Republic.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Obit_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Obit-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Maïnassara headed a <i>Conseil de Salut National</i> (National Salvation Council) composed of military officials which carried out a six-month transition period, during which a constitution was drafted and adopted on 12 May 1996.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Presidential campaigns were organised in the months that followed. Maïnassara entered the campaign as an independent candidate and won the <a href="/wiki/1996_Nigerien_parliamentary_election" title="1996 Nigerien parliamentary election">election</a> on 8 July 1996, the elections were viewed nationally and internationally by some as irregular, as the electoral commission was replaced during the campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Meanwhile, Maïnassara instigated an <a href="/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund" title="International Monetary Fund">IMF</a> and <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a>-approved privatisation programme which enriched some of his supporters and were opposed by the trade unions.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Following fraudulent local elections in 1999 the opposition ceased any cooperation with the Maïnassara regime.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In a circumstance (possibly attempting to flee the country), Maïnassara was assassinated at <a href="/wiki/Niamey_Airport" class="mw-redirect" title="Niamey Airport">Niamey Airport</a> on 9 April 1999.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Maj. <a href="/wiki/Daouda_Malam_Wank%C3%A9" title="Daouda Malam Wanké">Daouda Malam Wanké</a> then took over, establishing a transitional National Reconciliation Council to oversee the drafting of a constitution with a French-style <a href="/wiki/Semi-presidential_system" class="mw-redirect" title="Semi-presidential system">semi-presidential system</a>. This was adopted on 9 August 1999 and was followed by presidential and legislative <a href="/wiki/1999_Nigerien_general_election" title="1999 Nigerien general election">elections</a> in October and November of the same year.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> The elections were generally found to be free and fair by international observers. Wanké then withdrew from governmental affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Fifth_Republic_.281999.E2.80.932009.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Fifth_Republic_(1999–2009)">Fifth Republic (1999–2009)</span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:197px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nigerien_MNJ_fighter_technical_gun.JPG" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/04/Nigerien_MNJ_fighter_technical_gun.JPG" decoding="async" width="195" height="131" class="thumbimage" data-file-width="195" data-file-height="131" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nigerien_MNJ_fighter_technical_gun.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A <a href="/wiki/Tuareg_people" title="Tuareg people">Tuareg</a> rebel fighter in northern Niger during the Second Tuareg Rebellion, 2008</div></div></div>
<p>After winning the election in November 1999, President <a href="/wiki/Tandja_Mamadou" class="mw-redirect" title="Tandja Mamadou">Tandja Mamadou</a> was sworn in office on 22 December 1999 as the first president of the Fifth Republic. Mamadou brought about administrative and economic reforms that had been halted due to the military coups since the Third Republic, and helped peacefully resolve a decades-long boundary dispute with Benin.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> In August 2002, unrest within military camps occurred in <a href="/wiki/Niamey" title="Niamey">Niamey</a>, <a href="/wiki/Diffa" title="Diffa">Diffa</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Nguigmi" class="mw-redirect" title="Nguigmi">Nguigmi</a>, and the government was able to restore order within days. On 24 July 2004, municipal elections were held to elect local representatives, previously appointed by the government. These elections were followed by presidential elections, in which Mamadou was re-elected for a second term, thus becoming the first president of the republic to win consecutive elections without being deposed by military coups.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> The legislative and executive configuration remained somewhat similar to that of the first term of the president: <a href="/wiki/Hama_Amadou" title="Hama Amadou">Hama Amadou</a> was reappointed as prime minister and <a href="/wiki/Mahamane_Ousmane" title="Mahamane Ousmane">Mahamane Ousmane</a>, the head of the CDS party, was re-elected as the president of the National Assembly (parliament) by his peers.
</p><p>By 2007, the relationship between President Tandja Mamadou and his prime minister had "deteriorated", leading to the replacement of the latter in June 2007 by <a href="/wiki/Seyni_Oumarou" title="Seyni Oumarou">Seyni Oumarou</a> following a successful vote of no confidence at the Assembly.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> President Tandja Mamadou sought out to extend his presidency by modifying the constitution which limited presidential terms. Proponents of the extended presidency, rallied behind the 'Tazartche' (Hausa for 'overstay') movement, were countered by opponents ('anti-Tazartche') composed of opposition party militants and civil society activists.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The north saw the outbreak of a <a href="/wiki/Second_Tuareg_Rebellion" class="mw-redirect" title="Second Tuareg Rebellion">Second Tuareg Rebellion</a> in 2007 led by the <i><a href="/wiki/Niger_Movement_for_Justice" title="Niger Movement for Justice">Mouvement des Nigériens pour la justice</a></i> (MNJ). With a number of kidnappings the rebellion had "largely fizzled out inconclusively" by 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The "poor" security situation in the region is thought to have allowed elements of <a href="/wiki/Al-Qaeda_in_the_Islamic_Maghreb" title="Al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb">Al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb</a> (AQIM) to gain a foothold in the country.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Fourth_military_regime_.282009.E2.80.932010.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Fourth_military_regime_(2009–2010)">Fourth military regime (2009–2010)</span></h4>
<p>In 2009, President Tandja Mamadou decided to organize a constitutional referendum seeking to <a href="/wiki/2009%E2%80%932010_Nigerien_constitutional_crisis" title="2009–2010 Nigerien constitutional crisis">extend his presidency</a>, which was opposed by other political parties, and being against the decision of the Constitutional Court which had ruled that the referendum would be unconstitutional. Mamadou then modified and adopted a new constitution by referendum, which was declared illegal by the Constitutional Court, prompting Mamadou to dissolve the Court and assume emergency powers.<sup id="cite_ref-Reutersjune26_80-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Reutersjune26-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> The opposition boycotted the referendum and the constitution was adopted with 92.5% of voters and a 68% turnout, according to official results. The adoption of the constitution created a Sixth Republic, with a <a href="/wiki/Presidential_system" title="Presidential system">presidential system</a>, and the suspension of the 1999 Constitution and a three-year interim government with Tandja Mamadou as president. The events generated political and social unrest.<sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In a <a href="/wiki/2010_Nigerien_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="2010 Nigerien coup d&#39;état">coup d'état</a> in February 2010, a military junta led by captain <a href="/wiki/Salou_Djibo" title="Salou Djibo">Salou Djibo</a> was established in response to Tandja's attempted extension of his political term by modifying the constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Council_for_the_Restoration_of_Democracy" title="Supreme Council for the Restoration of Democracy">Supreme Council for the Restoration of Democracy</a>, led by General Salou Djibo, carried out a one-year transition plan, drafted a constitution and held elections in 2011.
</p>
<h4><span id="Seventh_Republic_.282010.E2.80.93.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Seventh_Republic_(2010–)">Seventh Republic (2010–)</span></h4>
<p>Following the adoption of a constitution in 2010 and <a href="/wiki/2011_Nigerien_general_election" title="2011 Nigerien general election">presidential elections</a> a year later, <a href="/wiki/Mahamadou_Issoufou" title="Mahamadou Issoufou">Mahamadou Issoufou</a> was elected as the first president of the Seventh Republic; he was then <a href="/wiki/2016_Nigerien_general_election" title="2016 Nigerien general election">re-elected</a> in 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-H-Dictionary_41-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-H-Dictionary-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The constitution restored the semi-presidential system which had been abolished a year earlier.
