An attempted coup against him in 2011 was thwarted and its ringleaders arrested.<sup id="cite_ref-FITWreport_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FITWreport-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> Issoufou's time in office has been marked by threats to the country's security, stemming from the fallout from the <a href="/wiki/Libyan_Civil_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Libyan Civil War">Libyan Civil War</a> and <a href="/wiki/Northern_Mali_conflict" class="mw-redirect" title="Northern Mali conflict">Northern Mali conflict</a>, a rise in attacks by AQIM, the use of Niger as a transit country for migrants (sometimes organised by criminal gangs), and the spillover of Nigeria's <a href="/wiki/Boko_Haram_insurgency" title="Boko Haram insurgency">Boko Haram insurgency</a> into south-eastern Niger.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> French and American forces are assisting Niger in countering these threats.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 27 December 2020, Nigeriens <a href="/wiki/2020%E2%80%9321_Nigerien_general_election" title="2020–21 Nigerien general election">went to the polls</a> after Issoufou announced he would step down, paving the way to a <a href="/wiki/Peaceful_transition_of_power" title="Peaceful transition of power">peaceful transition of power</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> No candidate won an absolute majority in the vote: <a href="/wiki/Mohamed_Bazoum" title="Mohamed Bazoum">Mohamed Bazoum</a> came closest with 39.33%. As per the constitution, a run-off election was held on 20 February 2021, with Bazoum taking 55.75% of the vote and opposition candidate (and former president) <a href="/wiki/Mahamane_Ousmane" title="Mahamane Ousmane">Mahamane Ousmane</a> taking 44.25%, according to the electoral commission.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 31 March 2021, Niger's security forces thwarted an attempted coup by a military unit in the capital, <a href="/wiki/Niamey" title="Niamey">Niamey</a>. Gunfire was heard in the presidential palace. The attack took place two days before newly elected president <a href="/wiki/Mohamed_Bazoum" title="Mohamed Bazoum">Mohamed Bazoum</a> was due to be sworn into office. The Presidential Guard arrested some people during the incident.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> On 2 April 2021, Bazoum was sworn in as the <a href="/wiki/President_of_Niger" class="mw-redirect" title="President of Niger">President of Niger</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Niger" title="Geography of Niger">Geography of Niger</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:252px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ng-map.png" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Ng-map.png/250px-Ng-map.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="267" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/1/1b/Ng-map.png 1.5x" data-file-width="329" data-file-height="352" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ng-map.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A map of Niger</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Niger_sat.png" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Niger_sat.png/220px-Niger_sat.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Niger_sat.png/330px-Niger_sat.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d2/Niger_sat.png/440px-Niger_sat.png 2x" data-file-width="1901" data-file-height="1421" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Niger_sat.png" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Satellite image of Niger</div></div></div>
<p>It is a <a href="/wiki/Landlocked" class="mw-redirect" title="Landlocked">landlocked</a> nation in West Africa located along the border between the <a href="/wiki/Sahara" title="Sahara">Sahara</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sub-Saharan_Africa" title="Sub-Saharan Africa">Sub-Saharan</a> regions. It borders <a href="/wiki/Nigeria" title="Nigeria">Nigeria</a> and <a href="/wiki/Benin" title="Benin">Benin</a> to the south, <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso" title="Burkina Faso">Burkina Faso</a> and <a href="/wiki/Mali" title="Mali">Mali</a> to the west, <a href="/wiki/Algeria" title="Algeria">Algeria</a> and <a href="/wiki/Libya" title="Libya">Libya</a> to the north and <a href="/wiki/Chad" title="Chad">Chad</a> to the east.
</p><p>It lies between latitudes <a href="/wiki/11th_parallel_north" title="11th parallel north">11°</a> and <a href="/wiki/24th_parallel_north" title="24th parallel north">24°N</a>, and longitudes <a href="/wiki/Prime_meridian" title="Prime meridian">0°</a> and <a href="/wiki/16th_meridian_east" title="16th meridian east">16°E</a>. Its area is 1,267,000 square kilometres (489,191&#160;sq&#160;mi) of which 300 square kilometres (116&#160;sq&#160;mi) is water. This makes it less than twice the size of <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">France</a>, and the world's 21st largest country.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It borders seven countries and has a total perimeter of 5,697 kilometres (3,540&#160;mi). The longest border is with <a href="/wiki/Nigeria" title="Nigeria">Nigeria</a> to the south (1,497&#160;km or 930&#160;mi). This is followed by <a href="/wiki/Chad" title="Chad">Chad</a> to the east, at 1,175&#160;km (730&#160;mi), <a href="/wiki/Algeria" title="Algeria">Algeria</a> to the north-northwest (956&#160;km or 594&#160;mi), and <a href="/wiki/Mali" title="Mali">Mali</a> at 821&#160;km (510&#160;mi). Niger has borders in its further southwest with <a href="/wiki/Burkina_Faso" title="Burkina Faso">Burkina Faso</a> at 628&#160;km (390&#160;mi) and <a href="/wiki/Benin" title="Benin">Benin</a> at 266&#160;km (165&#160;mi) and to the north-northeast <a href="/wiki/Libya" title="Libya">Libya</a> at 354&#160;km (220&#160;mi).
</p><p>The lowest point is the <a href="/wiki/Niger_River" title="Niger River">Niger River</a>, with an elevation of 200 metres (656&#160;ft). The highest point is <a href="/wiki/Mont_Idoukal-n-Tagh%C3%A8s" title="Mont Idoukal-n-Taghès">Mont Idoukal-n-Taghès</a> in the <a href="/wiki/A%C3%AFr_Mountains" title="Aïr Mountains">Aïr Mountains</a> at 2,022&#160;m (6,634&#160;ft).
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Climate">Climate</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Koppen-Geiger_Map_NER_present.svg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Koppen-Geiger_Map_NER_present.svg/220px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_NER_present.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="142" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Koppen-Geiger_Map_NER_present.svg/330px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_NER_present.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/62/Koppen-Geiger_Map_NER_present.svg/440px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_NER_present.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1313" data-file-height="850" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Koppen-Geiger_Map_NER_present.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Map of Köppen climate classification</div></div></div>
<p>The hotter and drier climate with <a href="/wiki/Desert" title="Desert">desert</a> area causes more frequent fires in some regions.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> In the south there is a tropical climate on the edges of the Niger River basin. The terrain is predominantly desert plains and <a href="/wiki/Sand_dune" class="mw-redirect" title="Sand dune">sand dunes</a>, with flat to rolling savanna in the south and hills in the north.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Biodiversity">Biodiversity</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Wildlife_of_Niger" title="Wildlife of Niger">Wildlife of Niger</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Elephant_dust_bath_park_w_niger.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Elephant_dust_bath_park_w_niger.jpg/220px-Elephant_dust_bath_park_w_niger.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Elephant_dust_bath_park_w_niger.jpg/330px-Elephant_dust_bath_park_w_niger.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Elephant_dust_bath_park_w_niger.jpg/440px-Elephant_dust_bath_park_w_niger.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="681" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Elephant_dust_bath_park_w_niger.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>An elephant in the <a href="/wiki/W_National_Park" title="W National Park">W National Park</a></div></div></div>
<p>The territory of Niger contains five terrestrial ecoregions: <a href="/wiki/Sahelian_Acacia_savanna" class="mw-redirect" title="Sahelian Acacia savanna">Sahelian Acacia savanna</a>, <a href="/wiki/West_Sudanian_savanna" title="West Sudanian savanna">West Sudanian savanna</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lake_Chad_flooded_savanna" title="Lake Chad flooded savanna">Lake Chad flooded savanna</a>, <a href="/wiki/South_Saharan_steppe_and_woodlands" title="South Saharan steppe and woodlands">South Saharan steppe and woodlands</a>, and <a href="/wiki/West_Saharan_montane_xeric_woodlands" title="West Saharan montane xeric woodlands">West Saharan montane xeric woodlands</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-DinersteinOlson2017_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The north is covered by deserts and semi-deserts. The typical mammal fauna consists of <a href="/wiki/Addax" title="Addax">addax</a> antelopes, <a href="/wiki/Scimitar-horned_oryx" class="mw-redirect" title="Scimitar-horned oryx">scimitar-horned oryx</a>, gazelles, and in the mountains, Barbary sheep. The <a href="/wiki/A%C3%AFr_and_T%C3%A9n%C3%A9r%C3%A9_National_Nature_Reserve" title="Aïr and Ténéré National Nature Reserve">Aïr and Ténéré National Nature Reserve</a> was founded in the northern parts to protect these species.
</p><p>The southern parts are naturally dominated savannahs. The <a href="/wiki/W_National_Park" title="W National Park">W National Park</a>, situated in the bordering area to Burkina Faso and <a href="/wiki/Benin" title="Benin">Benin</a>, belongs to "one of the most important areas" for wildlife in Western Africa, which is called the WAP (W–<a href="/wiki/Arli_National_Park" title="Arli National Park">Arli</a>–<a href="/wiki/Pendjari_National_Park" title="Pendjari National Park">Pendjari</a>) Complex. It has a population of the <a href="/wiki/West_African_lion" class="mw-redirect" title="West African lion">West African lion</a> and one of the last populations of the <a href="/wiki/Northwest_African_cheetah" title="Northwest African cheetah">Northwest African cheetah</a>.
</p><p>Other wildlife includes elephants, buffaloes, <a href="/wiki/Roan_antelopes" class="mw-redirect" title="Roan antelopes">roan antelopes</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kob" title="Kob">kob antelopes</a> and warthogs. The <a href="/wiki/West_African_giraffe" title="West African giraffe">West African giraffe</a> is found in the further north where it has its last relict population.
</p><p>Environmental issues include destructive farming practices as a result of population pressure, illegal hunting, bush fires in some areas and human encroachment upon the flood plains of the Niger River for paddy cultivation. Dams constructed on the Niger River in the neighboring countries of Mali and Guinea and within Niger are cited as a reason for a reduction of water flow in the Niger River—which has a direct effect upon the environment. A "lack of adequate staff" to guard wildlife in the parks and reserves is another factor cited for loss of wildlife.<sup id="cite_ref-Geels2006_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Geels2006-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Farmer-managed_natural_regeneration" title="Farmer-managed natural regeneration">Farmer-managed natural regeneration</a> is practiced since 1983 to increase food and timber production, and <a href="/wiki/Resilience_(ecology)" class="mw-redirect" title="Resilience (ecology)">resilience</a> to <a href="/wiki/Extremes_on_Earth" title="Extremes on Earth">climate extremes</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Governance_and_politics">Governance and politics</span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Niger" title="Politics of Niger">Politics of Niger</a> and <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Niger" title="Government of Niger">Government of Niger</a></div>
<p>Niger's new constitution was approved on 31 October 2010. It restored the semi-presidential system of government of the 1999 constitution (Fifth Republic) in which the president of the republic, elected by <a href="/wiki/Universal_suffrage" title="Universal suffrage">universal suffrage</a> for a five-year term, and a prime minister named by the president share <a href="/wiki/Executive_power" class="mw-redirect" title="Executive power">executive power</a>.
</p><p>On 26 May 2009, President Tandja dissolved parliament after the country's constitutional court ruled against plans to hold a referendum on whether to allow him a third term in office. According to the constitution, a new parliament was elected within three months.<sup id="cite_ref-96" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> This began a political struggle between Tandja, trying to extend his term-limited authority beyond 2009 through the establishment of a Sixth Republic, and his opponents who demanded that he step down at the end of his second term in December 2009. See <a href="/wiki/2009_Nigerien_constitutional_crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="2009 Nigerien constitutional crisis">2009 Nigerien constitutional crisis</a>. The military took over the country and President Tandja was put in prison, charged with corruption.
</p><p>The military kept their promise to return the country to democratic civilian rule. A constitutional referendum and national elections were held. A presidential election was held on 31 January 2011, but as no clear winner emerged, run-off elections were held on 12 March 2011. <a href="/wiki/Mahamadou_Issoufou" title="Mahamadou Issoufou">Mahamadou Issoufou</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Nigerien_Party_for_Democracy_and_Socialism" title="Nigerien Party for Democracy and Socialism">Nigerien Party for Democracy and Socialism</a> was elected president. A parliamentary election was held at the same time.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-98" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-99" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_relations">Foreign relations</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:202px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Drapeau_Niger_ambassade_Paris.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Drapeau_Niger_ambassade_Paris.jpg/200px-Drapeau_Niger_ambassade_Paris.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="266" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Drapeau_Niger_ambassade_Paris.jpg/300px-Drapeau_Niger_ambassade_Paris.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Drapeau_Niger_ambassade_Paris.jpg/400px-Drapeau_Niger_ambassade_Paris.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1738" data-file-height="2310" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Drapeau_Niger_ambassade_Paris.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Niger's flag waving at the embassy in Paris</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Niger" title="Foreign relations of Niger">Foreign relations of Niger</a></div>
<p>Niger pursues a moderate foreign policy and maintains friendly relations with the West and the Islamic world as well as non-aligned countries. It belongs to the UN and its main specialized agencies and in 1980–81 served on the <a href="/wiki/UN_Security_Council" class="mw-redirect" title="UN Security Council">UN Security Council</a>. Niger maintains a special relationship with former colonial power France and has close relations with its West African neighbors.
</p><p>It is a charter member of the <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a> and the <a href="/wiki/West_African_Monetary_Union" class="mw-redirect" title="West African Monetary Union">West African Monetary Union</a> and also belongs to the <a href="/wiki/Niger_Basin_Authority" title="Niger Basin Authority">Niger Basin Authority</a> and <a href="/wiki/Lake_Chad_Basin_Commission" title="Lake Chad Basin Commission">Lake Chad Basin Commission</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Economic_Community_of_West_African_States" title="Economic Community of West African States">Economic Community of West African States</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement" title="Non-Aligned Movement">Non-Aligned Movement</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Organisation_of_Islamic_Cooperation" title="Organisation of Islamic Cooperation">Organisation of Islamic Cooperation</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Organization_for_the_Harmonization_of_Business_Law_in_Africa" class="mw-redirect" title="Organization for the Harmonization of Business Law in Africa">Organization for the Harmonization of Business Law in Africa</a> (<a href="/wiki/OHADA" title="OHADA">OHADA</a>). The westernmost regions of Niger are joined with contiguous regions of Mali and Burkina Faso under the <a href="/wiki/Liptako-Gourma_Authority" title="Liptako-Gourma Authority">Liptako-Gourma Authority</a>.
</p><p>The border dispute with Benin, inherited from colonial times and concerning inter alia <a href="/wiki/L%C3%A9t%C3%A9_Island" title="Lété Island">Lété Island</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Niger_River" title="Niger River">Niger River</a>, was solved by the <a href="/wiki/International_Court_of_Justice" title="International Court of Justice">International Court of Justice</a> in 2005 to Niger's advantage.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military">Military</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Niger_Armed_Forces" title="Niger Armed Forces">Niger Armed Forces</a></div>
<p>The Niger Armed Forces (Forces armées nigériennes) are the military and paramilitary forces of Niger, under the president as supreme commander. They consist of the Niger Army (Armée de Terre), the Niger Air Force (Armée de l'Air) and the auxiliary paramilitary forces, such as the <a href="/wiki/Gendarmerie_Nationale_(Niger)" title="Gendarmerie Nationale (Niger)">National Gendarmerie (Gendarmerie nationale)</a> and the <a href="/wiki/National_Guard_of_Niger" title="National Guard of Niger">National Guard (Garde Nationale)</a>. Both paramilitary forces are trained in military fashion and have some military responsibilities in wartime. In peace time their duties are mostly policing duties.
</p><p>The armed forces are composed of approximately 12,900 personnel, including 3,700 <a href="/wiki/Gendarmerie_Nationale_(Niger)" title="Gendarmerie Nationale (Niger)">gendarmes</a>, 3200 <a href="/wiki/National_Guard_of_Niger" title="National Guard of Niger">national guards</a>, 300 air force personnel, and 6,000 army personnel. The armed forces of Niger have been involved several military coups over the years with the most recent in 2010. Niger's armed forces have a long history of military cooperation with France and the United States. As of 2013<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Niger&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, Niamey is home to a U.S. drone base.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Judicial_system">Judicial system</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Judiciary_of_Niger" title="Judiciary of Niger">Judiciary of Niger</a></div>
<p>The current Judiciary of Niger was established with the creation of the Fourth Republic in 1999. The constitution of December 1992 was revised by national referendum on 12 May 1996 and, again, by referendum, revised to the current version on 18 July 1999. It is based on the <a href="/wiki/Code_Napoleon" class="mw-redirect" title="Code Napoleon">Code Napoleon</a> "<i><a href="/wiki/Inquisitorial_system" title="Inquisitorial system">Inquisitorial system</a></i>", established in Niger during French colonial rule and the 1960 Constitution of Niger. The Court of Appeals reviews questions of fact and law, while the Supreme Court reviews application of the law and constitutional questions. The High Court of Justice (HCJ) deals with cases involving senior government officials. The justice system also includes civil criminal courts, customary courts, traditional mediation, and a military court.<sup id="cite_ref-Sory_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Sory-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> The military court provides the same rights as civil criminal courts; however, customary courts do not. The military court cannot try civilians.<sup id="cite_ref-USHRR2008_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-USHRR2008-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Law_enforcement">Law enforcement</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Law_enforcement_in_Niger" title="Law enforcement in Niger">Law enforcement in Niger</a></div>
<p>Law enforcement in Niger is the responsibility of the Ministry of Defense through the <a href="/wiki/Gendarmerie_Nationale_(Niger)" title="Gendarmerie Nationale (Niger)">National Gendarmerie</a> and the Ministry of the Interior through the <a href="/wiki/National_Police_(Niger)" class="mw-redirect" title="National Police (Niger)">National Police</a> and the <a href="/wiki/National_Guard_of_Niger" title="National Guard of Niger">National Guard</a>. The <a href="/wiki/National_Police_(Niger)" class="mw-redirect" title="National Police (Niger)">National Police</a> is primarily responsible for law enforcement in urban areas. Outside big cities and in rural areas, this responsibility falls on the <a href="/wiki/Gendarmerie_Nationale_(Niger)" title="Gendarmerie Nationale (Niger)">National Gendarmerie</a> and the <a href="/wiki/National_Guard_of_Niger" title="National Guard of Niger">National Guard</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Government_finance">Government finance</span></h3>
<p>Government finance is derived revenue exports (Mining, oil and agricultural exports) as well as various forms of taxes collected by the government. In the past, foreign aid has contributed to large percentages of the budget. In 2013, Niger's government has adopted a zero-deficit budget of 1.279 trillion CFA francs ($2.53 billion) which is claimed to balance revenues and expenditures by an 11% reduction in the budget from the previous year.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The 2014 budget was 1.867 trillion CFA which is distributed as follows according to: public debt (76,703,692,000 CFA), personnel expenditures (210,979,633,960 CFA), operating expenditures (128,988,777,711 CFA); subsidies and transfers: 308,379,641,366 CFA) and Investment (1,142,513,658,712 CFA).<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_aid">Foreign aid</span></h4>
<p>The importance of external support for Niger's development is demonstrated by the fact that about 45% of the government's FY 2002 budget, including 80% of its capital budget, derives from donor resources.<sup id="cite_ref-ussd2009_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussd2009-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> The most important donors in Niger are France, the <a href="/wiki/European_Union" title="European Union">European Union</a>, the <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a>, the <a href="/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund" title="International Monetary Fund">International Monetary Fund</a>, and various <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a> agencies (<a href="/wiki/UNDP" class="mw-redirect" title="UNDP">UNDP</a>, <a href="/wiki/UNICEF" title="UNICEF">UNICEF</a>, <a href="/wiki/FAO" class="mw-redirect" title="FAO">FAO</a>, <a href="/wiki/World_Food_Program" class="mw-redirect" title="World Food Program">World Food Program</a>, and <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Population_Fund" title="United Nations Population Fund">United Nations Population Fund</a>).
</p><p>Other principal donors include the United States, Belgium, Germany, Switzerland, Canada, and <a href="/wiki/Saudi_Arabia" title="Saudi Arabia">Saudi Arabia</a>. While <a href="/wiki/USAID" class="mw-redirect" title="USAID">USAID</a> does not have an office in Niger, the United States is a major donor, contributing nearly $10 million each year to Niger's development. The U.S. also is a major partner in policy coordination in such areas as food security and HIV/AIDS.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Administrative_divisions">Administrative divisions</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:502px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Niger_administrative_divisions.svg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Niger_administrative_divisions.svg/500px-Niger_administrative_divisions.svg.png" decoding="async" width="500" height="353" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Niger_administrative_divisions.svg/750px-Niger_administrative_divisions.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Niger_administrative_divisions.svg/1000px-Niger_administrative_divisions.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1123" data-file-height="793" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Niger_administrative_divisions.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Administrative divisions of Niger</div></div></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Regions_of_Niger" title="Regions of Niger">Regions of Niger</a>, <a href="/wiki/Departments_of_Niger" title="Departments of Niger">Departments of Niger</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Communes_of_Niger" title="Communes of Niger">Communes of Niger</a></div>
<p>Niger is divided into 7 <a href="/wiki/Regions_of_Niger" title="Regions of Niger">Regions</a> and one capital district. These Regions are subdivided into 36 <a href="/wiki/Departments_of_Niger" title="Departments of Niger">departments</a>. The 36 Departments are currently broken down into Communes of varying types. As of 2006<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Niger&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup> there were 265 communes, including communes urbaines (Urban Communes: as subdivisions of major cities), communes rurales (Rural Communes), in sparsely populated areas and postes administratifs (Administrative Posts) for largely uninhabited desert areas or military zones.
</p><p>Rural communes may contain official villages and settlements, while Urban Communes are divided into quarters. Niger subvisions were renamed in 2002, in the implementation of a decentralisation project, first begun in 1998. Previously, Niger was divided into 7 Departments, 36 Arrondissements, and Communes. These subdivisions were administered by officials appointed by the national government. These offices will be replaced in the future by democratically elected councils at each level.
</p><p>The <i>pre-2002</i> departments (renamed as regions) and capital district are:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Agadez_Region" title="Agadez Region">Agadez Region</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Diffa_Region" title="Diffa Region">Diffa Region</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Dosso_Region" title="Dosso Region">Dosso Region</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Maradi_Region" title="Maradi Region">Maradi Region</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Tahoua_Region" title="Tahoua Region">Tahoua Region</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Tillab%C3%A9ri_Region" title="Tillabéri Region">Tillabéri Region</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Zinder_Region" title="Zinder Region">Zinder Region</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Niamey" title="Niamey">Niamey</a> <span style="font-size:85%;">(capital district)</span></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Largest_cities_and_towns">Largest cities and towns</span></h3>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Economy">Economy</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Niger" title="Economy of Niger">Economy of Niger</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Niger_Product_Exports_(2019).svg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Niger_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/220px-Niger_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="143" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Niger_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/330px-Niger_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Niger_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg/440px-Niger_Product_Exports_%282019%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1100" data-file-height="715" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Niger_Product_Exports_(2019).svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A proportional representation of Niger exports, 2019</div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Niamey_night.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/Niamey_night.jpg/220px-Niamey_night.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/Niamey_night.jpg/330px-Niamey_night.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/Niamey_night.jpg/440px-Niamey_night.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3008" data-file-height="2000" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Niamey_night.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Niamey" title="Niamey">Niamey</a>, Niger's capital and economic hub</div></div></div>
<p>The economy of Niger centers on subsistence crops, livestock, and some of the world's largest uranium deposits. Drought cycles, desertification, a 2.9% population growth rate, and the drop in world demand for uranium have undercut the economy.
</p><p>Niger shares a common currency, the <a href="/wiki/CFA_franc" title="CFA franc">CFA franc</a>, and a common central bank, the <a href="/wiki/Central_Bank_of_West_African_States" title="Central Bank of West African States">Central Bank of West African States</a> (BCEAO), with seven other members of the <a href="/wiki/West_African_Monetary_Union" class="mw-redirect" title="West African Monetary Union">West African Monetary Union</a>. Niger is also a member of the <a href="/wiki/Organization_for_the_Harmonization_of_Business_Law_in_Africa" class="mw-redirect" title="Organization for the Harmonization of Business Law in Africa">Organization for the Harmonization of Business Law in Africa</a> (OHADA).<sup id="cite_ref-ohada.com_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohada.com-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Echangeur_a_Niamey_la_Nuit.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Echangeur_a_Niamey_la_Nuit.jpg/220px-Echangeur_a_Niamey_la_Nuit.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="123" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Echangeur_a_Niamey_la_Nuit.jpg/330px-Echangeur_a_Niamey_la_Nuit.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Echangeur_a_Niamey_la_Nuit.jpg/440px-Echangeur_a_Niamey_la_Nuit.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1401" data-file-height="781" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Echangeur_a_Niamey_la_Nuit.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Niamey at night</div></div></div>
<p>In December 2000, Niger qualified for enhanced debt relief under the International Monetary Fund program for <a href="/wiki/Heavily_Indebted_Poor_Countries" class="mw-redirect" title="Heavily Indebted Poor Countries">Heavily Indebted Poor Countries</a> (HIPC) and concluded an agreement with the Fund for <a href="/wiki/Poverty_Reduction_and_Growth_Facility" title="Poverty Reduction and Growth Facility">Poverty Reduction and Growth Facility</a> (PRGF). Debt relief provided under the enhanced HIPC initiative significantly reduces Niger's annual debt service obligations, freeing funds for expenditures on basic health care, primary education, HIV/AIDS prevention, rural infrastructure, and other programs geared at poverty reduction.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:March%C3%A9_Dol%C3%A9.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/March%C3%A9_Dol%C3%A9.jpg/220px-March%C3%A9_Dol%C3%A9.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="114" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/March%C3%A9_Dol%C3%A9.jpg/330px-March%C3%A9_Dol%C3%A9.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/March%C3%A9_Dol%C3%A9.jpg/440px-March%C3%A9_Dol%C3%A9.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1829" data-file-height="951" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:March%C3%A9_Dol%C3%A9.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Dolé Market</div></div></div>
<p>In December 2005, it was announced that Niger had received 100% multilateral <a href="/wiki/Debt_relief" title="Debt relief">debt relief</a> from the <a href="/wiki/IMF" class="mw-redirect" title="IMF">IMF</a>, which translates into the forgiveness of approximately US$86&#160;million in debts to the IMF, excluding the remaining assistance under HIPC. Nearly half of the government's budget is derived from foreign donor resources. Future growth may be sustained by exploitation of oil, gold, coal, and other mineral resources. Uranium prices have recovered somewhat in the last few years. A drought and locust infestation in 2005 led to food shortages for as many as 2.5&#160;million Nigeriens.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Demographics">Demographics</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Niger" title="Demographics of Niger">Demographics of Niger</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:1997_275-15_young_Wodaabe_women.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/1997_275-15_young_Wodaabe_women.jpg/220px-1997_275-15_young_Wodaabe_women.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="190" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/1997_275-15_young_Wodaabe_women.jpg/330px-1997_275-15_young_Wodaabe_women.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/1997_275-15_young_Wodaabe_women.jpg/440px-1997_275-15_young_Wodaabe_women.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1151" data-file-height="995" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:1997_275-15_young_Wodaabe_women.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Fula_people" title="Fula people">Fulani</a> women with traditional <a href="/wiki/Face_tattoo" title="Face tattoo">facial tattoos</a></div></div></div>
<p>As of 2021<sup class="plainlinks noexcerpt noprint asof-tag update" style="display:none;"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Niger&amp;action=edit">&#91;update&#93;</a></sup>, the population of Niger was 25,252,722<sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-UN_WPP_2022_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UN_WPP_2022-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>. Expanding from a population of 3.4 million in 1960, Niger's population has rapidly increased with a current growth rate of 3.3% (7.1 children per mother).<sup id="cite_ref-ins-demographics_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ins-demographics-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This growth rate is one of the highest in the world and is a source of concern for the government and international agencies.<sup id="cite_ref-irin-demographics_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-irin-demographics-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> The population is predominantly young, with 49.2% under 15 years old and 2.7% over 65 years, and predominantly rural with only 21% living in urban areas.<sup id="cite_ref-ins-demographics_107-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ins-demographics-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A 2005 study<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Avoid_weasel_words" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:Avoid weasel words"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too vague attribution or weasel words. (May 2021)">which?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> stated that over 800,000 people (nearly 8% of the population) <a href="/wiki/Slavery_in_Niger" title="Slavery in Niger">in Niger are enslaved</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

</div>
</div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Hausa_people" title="Hausa people">Hausa people</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zarma_people" title="Zarma people">Zarma people</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tuareg_people" title="Tuareg people">Tuareg people</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fula_people" title="Fula people">Fula people</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kanuri_people" title="Kanuri people">Kanuri people</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tubu_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Tubu people">Tubu people</a>, <a href="/wiki/Diffa_Arabs" title="Diffa Arabs">Diffa Arabs</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Gurma_people" title="Gurma people">Gurma people</a></div>
<p>As in most West African countries, Niger has a wide variety of ethnic groups. The ethnic makeup of Niger in 2001 was as follows: <a href="/wiki/Hausa_people" title="Hausa people">Hausa</a> (55.4%), <a href="/wiki/Zarma_people" title="Zarma people">Zarma</a> &amp; <a href="/wiki/Songhay_people_(subgroup)" class="mw-redirect" title="Songhay people (subgroup)">Songhay</a> (21%), <a href="/wiki/Tuareg_people" title="Tuareg people">Tuareg</a> (9.3%), <a href="/wiki/Fula_people" title="Fula people">Fula</a> (<a href="/wiki/French_language" title="French language">French</a>: <i lang="fr">Peuls</i>; <a href="/wiki/Fula_language" title="Fula language">Fula</a>: <i lang="ff-Latn">Fulɓe</i>) (8.5%), <a href="/wiki/Kanuri_people" title="Kanuri people">Kanuri Manga</a> (4.7%), <a href="/wiki/Tubu_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Tubu people">Tubu</a> (0.4%), <a href="/wiki/Diffa_Arabs" title="Diffa Arabs">Arab</a> (0.4%), <a href="/wiki/Gurma_people" title="Gurma people">Gourmantche</a> (0.4%), other (0.1%).<sup id="cite_ref-ins-demographics_107-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ins-demographics-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Zarma_people" title="Zarma people">Zarma</a> and <a href="/wiki/Songhay_people_(subgroup)" class="mw-redirect" title="Songhay people (subgroup)">Songhay</a> dominate the Dosso, Tillabéri, and Niamey régions, the <a href="/wiki/Hausa_people" title="Hausa people">Hausa</a> dominate the Zinder, Maradi, and Tahoua regions, <a href="/wiki/Kanuri_people" title="Kanuri people">Kanuri Manga</a> dominate the Diffa region, and <a href="/wiki/Touaregs" class="mw-redirect" title="Touaregs">Tuaregs</a> dominate the Agadez region in Northern Niger.<sup id="cite_ref-auto3_114-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto3-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Languages">Languages</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Niger" title="Languages of Niger">Languages of Niger</a></div>
<p>French, inherited from the colonial period, is the <a href="/wiki/Official_language" title="Official language">official language</a>. It is spoken mainly as a second language by people who have received a formal western education and serves as the administrative language. Niger has been a member of the <a href="/wiki/Organisation_Internationale_de_la_Francophonie" class="mw-redirect" title="Organisation Internationale de la Francophonie">Organisation Internationale de la Francophonie</a> since 1970.
</p><p>Niger has ten recognized <a href="/wiki/National_language" title="National language">national languages</a>, namely <a href="/wiki/Arabic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabic language">Arabic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Buduma_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Buduma language">Buduma</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fula_language" title="Fula language">Fulfulde</a>, <a href="/wiki/Gurma_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Gurma language">Gourmanchéma</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hausa_language" title="Hausa language">Hausa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kanuri_language" title="Kanuri language">Kanuri</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zarma_language" title="Zarma language">Zarma</a> &amp; <a href="/wiki/Songhoyboro_Ciine" title="Songhoyboro Ciine">Songhay</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tuareg_languages" title="Tuareg languages">Tamasheq</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tasawaq_language" title="Tasawaq language">Tassawaq</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tebu_languages" title="Tebu languages">Tebu</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-axl.cefan.ulaval.ca_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-axl.cefan.ulaval.ca-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> Each is spoken as a first language primarily by the ethnic group with which it is associated.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Hausa and Zarma-Songhai, the two most spoken languages, are widely spoken throughout the country as first or second languages.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Niger" title="Religion in Niger">Religion in Niger</a></div>
<div class="barbox tright" style="overflow-x: auto;">
<div style="border:1px solid silver; font-size:88%; padding:0.4em; width:auto; background: white;">
<table style="text-align:left; border-collapse:collapse; width:100%;">
<tbody><tr style="background:#ddd"><th style="text-align:center;" colspan="5">Religion in Niger</th></tr>
<tr style="font-size:88%; height:4px;">
<td colspan="2" style="padding:0 4px; text-align:left;">religion</td>
<td style="width:100px; text-align:left;"></td>
<td colspan="2" style="padding:0 4px; width:1em; text-align:right;">percent<sup id="cite_ref-census_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-census-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" style="padding-left: 0.4em; padding-right: 0.4em; min-width: 8em;">Islam</td>
<td style="width: 100px; border-left: solid 1px silver; border-right: solid 1px silver;"><div style="background:darkgreen; width:99.3%; overflow: hidden;">&#8201;</div></td>
<td colspan="2" style="padding-left: 1.2em; padding-right: 0.4em; text-align: right;">99.3%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" style="padding-left: 0.4em; padding-right: 0.4em; min-width: 8em;">Christianity</td>
<td style="width: 100px; border-left: solid 1px silver; border-right: solid 1px silver;"><div style="background:purple; width:0.3%; overflow: hidden;">&#8201;</div></td>
<td colspan="2" style="padding-left: 1.2em; padding-right: 0.4em; text-align: right;">0.3%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" style="padding-left: 0.4em; padding-right: 0.4em; min-width: 8em;">Animism</td>
<td style="width: 100px; border-left: solid 1px silver; border-right: solid 1px silver;"><div style="background:blue; width:0.2%; overflow: hidden;">&#8201;</div></td>
<td colspan="2" style="padding-left: 1.2em; padding-right: 0.4em; text-align: right;">0.2%</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td colspan="2" style="padding-left: 0.4em; padding-right: 0.4em; min-width: 8em;">Irreligious</td>
<td style="width: 100px; border-left: solid 1px silver; border-right: solid 1px silver;"><div style="background:black; width:0.1%; overflow: hidden;">&#8201;</div></td>
<td colspan="2" style="padding-left: 1.2em; padding-right: 0.4em; text-align: right;">0.1%</td>
</tr>

</tbody></table>
</div>
</div>
<p>Niger is a <a href="/wiki/Secular_country" class="mw-redirect" title="Secular country">secular country</a> and separation of state and religion is guaranteed by Articles 3 and 175 of the 2010 Constitution, which dictate that future amendments or revisions may not modify the secular nature of the republic of Niger. <a href="/wiki/Religious_freedom" class="mw-redirect" title="Religious freedom">Religious freedom</a> is protected by Article 30 of the same constitution. <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a>, widespread in the region since the 10th century, has greatly shaped the culture and mores of the people of Niger. Islam is the most dominant religion, practiced by 99.3% of the population according to the 2012 census.<sup id="cite_ref-census_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-census-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The other two main religions of Niger are <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a>, practiced by 0.3% of the population, and Animism (<a href="/wiki/African_traditional_religion" class="mw-redirect" title="African traditional religion">traditional indigenous religious beliefs</a>), practiced by 0.2% of the population.<sup id="cite_ref-census_3-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-census-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Christianity was established earlier in the country by missionaries during the French colonial years. Other urban Christian expatriate communities from Europe and West Africa are also present. Religious persecution has flared in recent years in Niger; Christian charity Open Doors now lists Niger as the 37th most difficult country in which to be a Christian on their <a href="/wiki/World_Watch_List" class="mw-redirect" title="World Watch List">World Watch List</a>, 'reflecting how pressure is increasing on Christians in this [...] nation.'<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> Relations between Muslims and Christians have generally been cordial, according to the respective representatives of Christian and Muslim groups in Niger.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The numbers of Animist practitioners are a point of contention. As recently as the late 19th century, much of the south center of the nation was unreached by Islam, and the conversion of some rural areas has been only partial. There are still areas where animist based festivals and traditions (such as the <a href="/wiki/Bori_(religion)" class="mw-redirect" title="Bori (religion)">Bori religion</a>) are practiced by <a href="/wiki/Syncretic" class="mw-redirect" title="Syncretic">syncretic</a> Muslim communities (in some Hausa areas as well as among some <a href="/wiki/Toubou" class="mw-redirect" title="Toubou">Toubou</a> and <a href="/wiki/Wodaabe" title="Wodaabe">Wodaabe</a> pastoralists), as opposed to several small communities who maintain their pre-Islamic religion. These include the Hausa-speaking <a href="/wiki/Maouri_people" title="Maouri people">Maouri</a> (or <i>Azna</i>, the Hausa word for "pagan") community in <a href="/wiki/Dogondoutci" class="mw-redirect" title="Dogondoutci">Dogondoutci</a> in the south-southwest and the <a href="/wiki/Kanuri_language" title="Kanuri language">Kanuri</a> speaking Manga near <a href="/wiki/Zinder" title="Zinder">Zinder</a>, both of whom practice variations of the pre-Islamic Hausa <a href="/wiki/Maguzawa" class="mw-redirect" title="Maguzawa">Maguzawa</a> religion. There are also some tiny Boudouma and Songhay animist communities in the southwest.<sup id="cite_ref-Decalo79_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Decalo79-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> Over the past decade, syncretic practices have become less common among Muslim Nigerien communities.<sup id="cite_ref-:2_118-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Islam">Islam</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_Niger" title="Islam in Niger">Islam in Niger</a></div>
<p>The majority of Muslims in Niger are <a href="/wiki/Sunni" class="mw-redirect" title="Sunni">Sunni</a>, 7% are <a href="/wiki/Shi%27a" class="mw-redirect" title="Shi&#39;a">Shi'a</a>, 5% are <a href="/wiki/Ahmadiyya" title="Ahmadiyya">Ahmadiyya</a> and 20% <a href="/wiki/Non-denominational" title="Non-denominational">non-denominational</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-pew_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pew-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-report_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-report-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> Islam was spread into what is now Niger beginning in the 15th century, by both the expansion of the <a href="/wiki/Songhai_Empire" title="Songhai Empire">Songhai Empire</a> in the west, and the influence of the <a href="/wiki/Trans-Saharan_trade" title="Trans-Saharan trade">Trans-Saharan trade</a> traveling from the <a href="/wiki/Maghreb" title="Maghreb">Maghreb</a> and <a href="/wiki/Egypt" title="Egypt">Egypt</a>. <a href="/wiki/Tuareg_people" title="Tuareg people">Tuareg</a> expansion from the north, culminating in their seizure of the far eastern oases from the <a href="/wiki/Bornu_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Bornu Empire">Kanem–Bornu Empire</a> in the 17th centuries, spread distinctively <a href="/wiki/Berber_mythology" class="mw-redirect" title="Berber mythology">Berber</a> practices.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Niger,_Filingu%C3%A9_(25),_street_scene_with_mosque.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Niger%2C_Filingu%C3%A9_%2825%29%2C_street_scene_with_mosque.jpg/220px-Niger%2C_Filingu%C3%A9_%2825%29%2C_street_scene_with_mosque.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="86" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Niger%2C_Filingu%C3%A9_%2825%29%2C_street_scene_with_mosque.jpg/330px-Niger%2C_Filingu%C3%A9_%2825%29%2C_street_scene_with_mosque.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Niger%2C_Filingu%C3%A9_%2825%29%2C_street_scene_with_mosque.jpg/440px-Niger%2C_Filingu%C3%A9_%2825%29%2C_street_scene_with_mosque.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4356" data-file-height="1704" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Niger,_Filingu%C3%A9_(25),_street_scene_with_mosque.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Small mosque in <a href="/wiki/Filingue" title="Filingue">Filingue</a></div></div></div>
<p>Both <a href="/wiki/Zarma_people" title="Zarma people">Zarma</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hausa_people" title="Hausa people">Hausa</a> areas were greatly influenced by the 18th- and 19th-century <a href="/wiki/Fula_jihads" title="Fula jihads">Fula led</a> <a href="/wiki/Sufi" class="mw-redirect" title="Sufi">Sufi</a> brotherhoods, most notably the <a href="/wiki/Sokoto_Caliphate" title="Sokoto Caliphate">Sokoto Caliphate</a> (in today's Nigeria). Modern Muslim practice in Niger is often tied to the <a href="/wiki/Tijaniya" class="mw-redirect" title="Tijaniya">Tijaniya</a> <a href="/wiki/Sufism" title="Sufism">Sufi</a> <a href="/wiki/Tariqah" class="mw-redirect" title="Tariqah">brotherhoods</a>, although there are small minority groups tied to <a href="/wiki/Hammallism" class="mw-redirect" title="Hammallism">Hammallism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nyassist" class="mw-redirect" title="Nyassist">Nyassist</a> Sufi orders in the west, and the <a href="/wiki/Sanusiya" class="mw-redirect" title="Sanusiya">Sanusiya</a> in the far northeast.<sup id="cite_ref-Decalo79_119-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Decalo79-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A small center of followers of Salafi movement within Sunni Islam have appeared in the last thirty years, in the capital and in <a href="/wiki/Maradi,_Niger" title="Maradi, Niger">Maradi</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> These small groups, linked to similar groups in <a href="/wiki/Jos" title="Jos">Jos</a>, Nigeria, came to public prominence in the 1990s during a series of religious riots.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite this, Niger maintains a tradition as a <a href="/wiki/Secular_state" title="Secular state">secular state</a>, protected by law.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> Interfaith relations are deemed very good, and the forms of Islam traditionally practiced in most of the country are marked by tolerance of other faiths and lack of restrictions on personal freedom.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> Alcohol, such as the locally produced Bière Niger, is sold openly in most of the country.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Niger" title="Education in Niger">Education in Niger</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Niger_primary_school_MCC3500.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Niger_primary_school_MCC3500.jpg/220px-Niger_primary_school_MCC3500.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="127" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Niger_primary_school_MCC3500.jpg/330px-Niger_primary_school_MCC3500.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Niger_primary_school_MCC3500.jpg/440px-Niger_primary_school_MCC3500.jpg 2x" data-file-width="500" data-file-height="289" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Niger_primary_school_MCC3500.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A primary classroom in Niger</div></div></div>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Literacy_rate" class="mw-redirect" title="Literacy rate">literacy rate</a> of Niger is among the lowest in the world; in 2005 it was estimated to be only 28.7% (42.9% male and 15.1% female).<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> Primary education in Niger is compulsory for six years.<sup id="cite_ref-ilab_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ilab-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> The primary school enrollment and attendance rates are low, particularly for girls.<sup id="cite_ref-ilab_128-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ilab-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> In 1997, the gross primary enrollment rate was 29.3 percent, and in 1996, the net primary enrollment rate was 24.5 percent.<sup id="cite_ref-ilab_128-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ilab-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>About 60 percent of children who finish primary schools are boys, as the majority of girls rarely attend school for more than a few years.<sup id="cite_ref-ilab_128-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ilab-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Children are often forced to work rather than attend school, particularly during planting or <a href="/wiki/Harvest" title="Harvest">harvest</a> periods.<sup id="cite_ref-ilab_128-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ilab-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Nomad" title="Nomad">Nomadic</a> children in the north of the country often do not have access to schools.<sup id="cite_ref-ilab_128-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ilab-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Health">Health</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Health_in_Niger" title="Health in Niger">Health in Niger</a></div>
<p>The child mortality rate in Niger (deaths among children between the ages of 1 and 4) is high (248 per 1,000) due to generally poor health conditions and inadequate nutrition for most of the country's children. According to the organization <a href="/wiki/Save_the_Children" title="Save the Children">Save the Children</a>, Niger has the world's highest <a href="/wiki/Infant_mortality_rate" class="mw-redirect" title="Infant mortality rate">infant mortality rate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:HOPITAL_DE_REFERENCE_DE_MARADI.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/HOPITAL_DE_REFERENCE_DE_MARADI.jpg/220px-HOPITAL_DE_REFERENCE_DE_MARADI.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="111" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/HOPITAL_DE_REFERENCE_DE_MARADI.jpg/330px-HOPITAL_DE_REFERENCE_DE_MARADI.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/HOPITAL_DE_REFERENCE_DE_MARADI.jpg/440px-HOPITAL_DE_REFERENCE_DE_MARADI.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1234" data-file-height="624" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:HOPITAL_DE_REFERENCE_DE_MARADI.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Maradi Reference Hospital</div></div></div>
<p>Niger also has the highest <a href="/wiki/Fertility_rate" class="mw-redirect" title="Fertility rate">fertility rate</a> in the world (6.49 births per woman according to 2017 estimates);<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> this has resulted in nearly half (49.7%) of the Nigerien population being under age 15 in 2020.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> Niger has the 11th highest <a href="/wiki/Maternal_mortality" class="mw-redirect" title="Maternal mortality">maternal mortality</a> rate in the world at 820 deaths/100,000 live births.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> There were 3 physicians and 22 nurses per 100,000 persons in 2006.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clean drinking water is scarce by global standards, with significant differences between urban and rural areas. Niger is often located at the bottom of the <a href="/wiki/Human_Development_Index" title="Human Development Index">UN Human Development Index</a>. Roughly 92% of the population lives in rural areas in the Tillabéri region along the western frontier, and there is a chronic scarcity of clean water, particularly during the hot season, when temperatures regularly exceed 40 degrees Celsius.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Just 40% of the 30,000 inhabitants in <a href="/wiki/T%C3%A9ra" title="Téra">Téra</a>, a city northwest of the country's capital of Niamey and near to the Burkina Faso border, have access to a working public water infrastructure.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_134-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> Société de Patrimoine des Eaux du Niger (SPEN), Niger's water authority, opened ten boreholes and built a water treatment plant in 2018 to provide potable water to <a href="/wiki/T%C3%A9ra" title="Téra">Téra</a> and the surrounding areas. The water supply ran out about a year later, and the water treatment facility was forced to close.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_134-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With the help of a donation fund from the Dutch government, the <a href="/wiki/European_Investment_Bank" title="European Investment Bank">European Investment Bank</a> is collaborating with the Niger water authority to find solutions to Niger's water issues. The <a href="/wiki/World_Bank" title="World Bank">World Bank</a> identified Niger as one of the 18 fragile regions of <a href="/wiki/Sub-Saharan_Africa" title="Sub-Saharan Africa">Sub-Saharan Africa</a>. The EU bank has a history of investing in regions like these.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The European Investment Bank and the Niger Water Authority are looking at two options for dealing with Téra's water shortages. The first choice is to repair the water tank on the outskirts of town. Another choice is to treat and transport water from the Niger River, which is located more than 100 kilometres to the east. Villages between Téra and the <a href="/wiki/Niger_River" title="Niger River">Niger River</a> will also have access to sewage. The European Investment Bank will also look at renewable energy as a way to save costs.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_140-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Niger" title="Culture of Niger">Culture of Niger</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Zinder_sultans_horsemen_festival.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Zinder_sultans_horsemen_festival.jpg/220px-Zinder_sultans_horsemen_festival.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Zinder_sultans_horsemen_festival.jpg/330px-Zinder_sultans_horsemen_festival.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Zinder_sultans_horsemen_festival.jpg/440px-Zinder_sultans_horsemen_festival.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="533" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Zinder_sultans_horsemen_festival.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Horsemen at the traditional <a href="/wiki/Ramadan" title="Ramadan">Ramadan</a> festival at the <a href="/wiki/Sultanate_of_Damagaram" title="Sultanate of Damagaram">Sultan's</a> Palace in the <a href="/wiki/Hausa_people" title="Hausa people">Hausa</a> city of <a href="/wiki/Zinder" title="Zinder">Zinder</a></div></div></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ancient_home_zinder_niger.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Ancient_home_zinder_niger.jpg/220px-Ancient_home_zinder_niger.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Ancient_home_zinder_niger.jpg/330px-Ancient_home_zinder_niger.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/Ancient_home_zinder_niger.jpg/440px-Ancient_home_zinder_niger.jpg 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="533" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ancient_home_zinder_niger.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A traditional home in <a href="/wiki/Zinder" title="Zinder">Zinder</a></div></div></div>
<p>Nigerien culture is marked by variation, evidence of the cultural crossroads which <a href="/wiki/French_colonial_empires" class="mw-redirect" title="French colonial empires">French colonialism</a> formed into a unified state from the beginning of the 20th century. What is now Niger was created from four distinct cultural areas in the pre-colonial era: the <a href="/wiki/Zarma_people" title="Zarma people">Zarma</a> and <a href="/wiki/Songhay_people_(subgroup)" class="mw-redirect" title="Songhay people (subgroup)">Songhai</a> dominated the <a href="/wiki/Niger_River" title="Niger River">Niger River</a> valley in the southwest; the northern periphery of <a href="/wiki/Hausaland" class="mw-redirect" title="Hausaland">Hausaland</a>, made mostly of those states which had resisted the <a href="/wiki/Sokoto_Caliphate" title="Sokoto Caliphate">Sokoto Caliphate</a>, and ranged along the long southern border with Nigeria; the <a href="/wiki/Chad_basin" class="mw-redirect" title="Chad basin">Lake Chad basin</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kaouar" title="Kaouar">Kaouar</a> in the far east, populated by <a href="/wiki/Kanuri_people" title="Kanuri people">Kanuri</a> farmers and <a href="/wiki/Toubou" class="mw-redirect" title="Toubou">Toubou</a> pastoralists who had once been part of the <a href="/wiki/Bornu_Empire" class="mw-redirect" title="Bornu Empire">Kanem–Bornu Empire</a>; and the <a href="/wiki/Tuareg_people" title="Tuareg people">Tuareg</a> nomads of the <a href="/wiki/A%C3%AFr_Mountains" title="Aïr Mountains">Aïr Mountains</a> and the Sahara in the vast north.
</p><p>Each of these communities, along with smaller ethnic groups like the pastoral <a href="/wiki/Wodaabe" title="Wodaabe">Wodaabe</a> <a href="/wiki/Fula_people" title="Fula people">Fula</a>, brought their own cultural traditions to the new state of Niger. While successive post-independence governments have tried to forge a shared national culture, this has been slow forming, in part because the major Nigerien communities have their own cultural histories, and in part because Nigerien ethnic groups such as the <a href="/wiki/Hausa_people" title="Hausa people">Hausa</a>, Tuareg and Kanuri are but part of larger ethnic communities which cross borders introduced under colonialism.
</p><p>Until the 1990s, government and politics was inordinately dominated by <a href="/wiki/Niamey" title="Niamey">Niamey</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Zarma_people" title="Zarma people">Zarma people</a> of the surrounding region. At the same time the plurality of the population, in the Hausa borderlands between <a href="/wiki/Birni-N%27Konni" title="Birni-N&#39;Konni">Birni-N'Konni</a> and <a href="/wiki/Maine-Soroa" class="mw-redirect" title="Maine-Soroa">Maine-Soroa</a>, have often looked culturally more to Hausaland in Nigeria than Niamey. Between 1996 and 2003, primary school attendance was around 30%,<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> including 36% of males and only 25% of females. Additional education occurs through <a href="/wiki/Madrasa" title="Madrasa">madrasas</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Festivals_and_cultural_events">Festivals and cultural events</span></h3>
<h4><span id="Gu.C3.A9rewol_festival"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Guérewol_festival">Guérewol festival</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Gu%C3%A9rewol" title="Guérewol">Guérewol</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:202px;"><a href="/wiki/File:1997_274-5_Gerewol.jpg" class="image"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/1997_274-5_Gerewol.jpg/200px-1997_274-5_Gerewol.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="299" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/1997_274-5_Gerewol.jpg/300px-1997_274-5_Gerewol.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/1997_274-5_Gerewol.jpg/400px-1997_274-5_Gerewol.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1043" data-file-height="1560" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:1997_274-5_Gerewol.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Participants in the <a href="/wiki/Gu%C3%A9rewol" title="Guérewol">Guérewol</a> perform the <i>Guérewol</i> dance, 1997.</div></div></div>
<p>The Guérewol festival is a traditional Wodaabe cultural event that takes place in <a href="/wiki/Abalak" title="Abalak">Abalak</a> in <a href="/wiki/Tahoua_Region" title="Tahoua Region">Tahoua region</a> or <a href="/wiki/In-Gall" title="In-Gall">In'Gall</a> in <a href="/wiki/Agadez_Region" title="Agadez Region">Agadez Region</a>. It is an annual traditional courtship ritual practiced by the Wodaabe (Fula) people of Niger. During this ceremony, young men dressed in elaborate ornamentation and made up in traditional face painting gather in lines to dance and sing, vying for the attention of marriageable young women. The Guérewol festival is an international attraction and was featured in films and magazines as prominent as the <a href="/wiki/National_Geographic_(magazine)" class="mw-redirect" title="National Geographic (magazine)">National Geographic</a>.
</p>
<h4><span id="Cure_Sal.C3.A9e_festival"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Cure_Salée_festival">Cure Salée festival</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Cure_Salee" title="Cure Salee">Cure Salee</a></div>
<p>"La Cure salée" (English: Salt Cure) is a yearly festival of Tuareg and Wodaabe nomads in <a href="/wiki/In-Gall" title="In-Gall">In'Gall</a> in <a href="/wiki/Agadez_Region" title="Agadez Region">Agadez Region</a> traditionally to celebrate the end of the rainy season. For three days, the festival features a parade of <a href="/wiki/Tuareg" class="mw-redirect" title="Tuareg">Tuareg</a> camel riders followed with camel and horse races, songs, dances, and storytelling.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Media">Media</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Media_of_Niger" class="mw-redirect" title="Media of Niger">Media of Niger</a></div>
<p>Niger began developing diverse media in the late 1990s. Prior to the Third Republic, Nigeriens only had access to tightly controlled state media.<sup id="cite_ref-sainforeport2002fr_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sainforeport2002fr-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> Now Niamey contains scores of newspapers and magazines; some, like <i>Le Sahel</i>, are government operated, while many are critical of the government.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Radio is the most important medium, as television sets are beyond the buying power of many of the rural poor, and illiteracy prevents print media from becoming a mass medium.<sup id="cite_ref-Geels2006_94-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Geels2006-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In addition to the national and regional radio services of the state broadcaster <a href="/wiki/Office_of_Radio_and_Television_of_Niger" title="Office of Radio and Television of Niger">ORTN</a>, there are four privately owned radio networks which total more than 100 stations. Three of them—the <a href="/wiki/Anfani_FM" title="Anfani FM">Anfani Group</a>, Sarounia and Tenere—are urban-based commercial-format <a href="/wiki/FM_broadcasting" title="FM broadcasting">FM</a> networks in the major towns.<sup id="cite_ref-ussd9506_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussd9506-145">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> There is also a network of over 80 community radio stations spread across all seven regions of the country, governed by the Comité de Pilotage de Radios de Proximité (CPRP), a civil society organisation. The independent-sector radio networks are collectively estimated by CPRP officials to cover some 7.6&#160;million people, or about 73% of the population (2005).
</p><p>Aside from Nigerien radio stations, the <a href="/wiki/BBC" title="BBC">BBC</a>'s Hausa service is listened to on FM repeaters across wide parts of the country, particularly in the south, close to the border with Nigeria. <a href="/wiki/Radio_France_Internationale" title="Radio France Internationale">Radio France Internationale</a> also rebroadcasts in French through some of the commercial stations, via satellite. Tenere FM also runs a national independent television station of the same name.<sup id="cite_ref-ussd9506_145-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ussd9506-145">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite relative freedom at the national level, Nigerien <a href="/wiki/Journalism" title="Journalism">journalists</a> say they are often pressured by local authorities.<sup id="cite_ref-issa04_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-issa04-146">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> The state ORTN network depends financially on the government, partly through a surcharge on electricity bills, and partly through direct subsidy. The sector is governed by the <a href="/wiki/High_Council_for_Communication_(Niger)" title="High Council for Communication (Niger)">Conseil Supérieur de Communications</a>, established as an independent body in the early 1990s, since 2007 headed by <a href="/w/index.php?title=Daouda_Diallo&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Daouda Diallo (page does not exist)">Daouda Diallo</a>. International human rights groups have criticised the government since at least 1996 as using regulation and police to punish criticism of the state.<sup id="cite_ref-cpj2006_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cpj2006-147">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span></h2>
